## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Prolonged droughts across the country have accelerated vegetation loss in desert environments, reducing ecosystem services related to sediment stabilisation and water infiltration. As a result, the rate of dune advancement along the Sahara-Sahel boundary has increased and greater sand encroachment has been observed in oases, with subsequent impacts on water resources, agricultural productivity and income generation across northern Mauritania.

Siltation of dams, boreholes, wells and rivers has reduced the accessibility and availability of already scarce water resources in the region. Moreover, reduced rainfall and water infiltration resulting from vegetation loss have increased the depth of the water table. This has led to many pastoral wells drying up, impacting pastoralists’ abilities to maintain nomadic lifestyles. Consequently, many nomadic pastoralists are adopting sedentary lifestyles around rural oases, increasing pressure on limited natural resources such as the demand for food, water and income to support rural-urban communities growth.

Sand inundation is threatening important socio-economic infrastructure such as roads, schools and hospitals located within the Sahel-Sahara region. Indeed, in extreme cases, sand encroachment has resulted in the displacement of entire villages and communities. These processes will continue to worsen, with the Sahara Desert expected to spread southwards into the country's most fertile lands in the Sahelian region and near the Senegal River.

To slow the rate of desertification and enhance water security in Mauritania, the restoration and conservation of ecosystems along the Sahel-Saharan boundary is required to enhance ecosystem services relating to dune stabilisation, water infiltration and the provision of natural resources. Four regional hubs, where desertification processes are most severe and adaptation needs are greatest, have been identified in northern Mauritania. These hubs include: i) Aoujeft, in the Adrar province; ii) Rachid, in the Tagant province; iii) Tamcheket, in the Hodh El Gharbi province; and iv) N ema, in the Hodh El Chargui province.

# B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Climate change is profoundly impacting Mauritanian communities, particularly those living along the Sahe l-Saharan boundary that extends from the northwest to the southeast of the country. A noticeable impact of climate change-induced desertification is the accelerated advance of sand dunes. Dams, boreholes, wells and rivers are becoming increasingly silted as a result of sand inundation, reducing access to and the availability of already-strained water resources (see Figure 60 in Annex 2). Arable land, rangeland and pastures are being inundated, severely compromising the agricultural (see Figure 27 in Annex 2) and livestock practices in the oases. In addition, sand inundation threatens critical socio-economic infrastructure, including, inter alia, roads, school buildings and hospitals. In extreme cases, sand dunes have displaced entire villages, in some cases more than once (see Figure 15 in Annex 2). The ongoing decline in the water table has also dried up wells commonly used by pastoralist herders, impacting their ability to practice transhumance.

Combined with the climate change-induced loss of rangelands and pastures, water scarcity has contributed to previously nomadic herders becoming more sedentary around rural oases where resource access is reliable. However, sedentarisation increases pressure on limited resources, creating a source of conflict — particularly between farmers and herders.

Four regional hubs have been identified along the boundary where desertification processes are most severe and the need for adaptation is greatest. These hubs are: i) Aoujeft, in the wilayah of Adrar; ii) Rachid, in the wilyah of Tagant; iii) Tamcheket, in the wilayah of Hodh El Gharbi; and iv) Nema, in the wilayah of Hodh El Chargui. Each hub covers an area of 80–85 km², spans several communes and comprises an urban centre surrounded by rural hinterland. The locations of the four hubs — Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema.

Aoujeft hub comprises six communes with a total population of ~35,600; Rachid hub spans three communes and sustains ~25,600 people; Tamcheket hub covers five communes and houses ~43,300 inhabitants; and Nema hub encompasses ten communes with a total population of ~99,600.

## Climate science basis

### Observed climate change

Observations of historical climate trends show that the mean annual temperature in Mauritania has increased by 0.3°C per decade over the last 70 years — from 26.10°C in 1950 to 28.46°C in 2020. Although warming has been observed across all major climatic zones in the country, temperature increases have been greatest over the Sahara Desert and lowest over the Senegal River Headwaters (Error! Reference source not found). Moreover, the latest available 30-year weather station data (1981–2010) indicate that, in general, temperatures have also increased within the focal hubs of Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema (see Table 27 in Annex 2 for observed temperature changes in the four focal hubs).

In addition to shifting temperatures, climate change in Mauritania has induced: i) an increase in the frequency of extreme rainfall events and associated flash floods; and ii) a general decline in low-intensity, long-duration precipitation events (see Table 32 in Annex 2 for observed precipitation trends in the four focal hubs). Consequently, while heavy precipitation events have become more intense, rainfall is increasingly concentrated over shorter time periods. This shift has resulted in increased rainfall irregularity and prolonged dry spells across the region.

Precipitation has decreased during the wet season and over the entire year in Nema, Tamcheket and Rachid. In contrast, the Aoujeft hub has undergone a small but statistically significant increase in its overall precipitation. Despite the overall decline in average precipitation, an increase in extreme daily rainfall, which often results in flash floods, has been observed in all four hubs over both time frames.

# Annex 2 — Appendix 2: Trend Analysis Report for Mauritania

## Climate Change Risk Assessment for Mauritania

The Standardised Precipitation-Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI) is an indicator of drought severity that accounts for precipitation and potential evapotranspiration. Values +2 or higher indicate extremely wet years, while values -2 or less indicate extremely dry years.

The IPCC AR6 identifies five potential scenarios based on emission levels and Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSPs) that are used to predict future climate change trends. SSP1-2.6 describes a 'green road' scenario whereby the world shifts towards a sustainable path, with CO2 emissions reaching net zero by 2075. SSP2-4.5 describes a 'middle of the road' scenario whereby emissions remain around current levels before falling around the mid-century as socioeconomic factors follow historic trends, with slow progress towards sustainability. SSP5-8.5 describes a worst-case scenario whereby CO2 emissions double by 2050, with rapid economic growth dependent on fossil fuels and energy-intensive lifestyles.

## References

Zha J, Shen C, Li Z, Wu J, Zhao D, Wenxuan F, Sun M, Azorin-Molina C & Deng K. 2021. Projected changes in global terrestrial near-surface wind speed in 1.5–4.0°C global warming levels. Environmental Research Letters. DOI: 10.1088/1748-9326/ac2fdd.

## Observed Potential Evapotranspiration (ETP)

Observed potential evapotranspiration (ETP) values are high in all four hubs (1,700 –2,000 mm/year), with the highest values observed in Nema and Tamcheket. In addition, each hub has negative differences between precipitation and evapotranspiration (P-ETP), indicating that water loss via ETP exceeds water gained via precipitation.

## Sand Movements

Sand movements are driven by the prevalence of strong winds from the north of Mauritania including: i) the north-eastern trade winds; and ii) the harmattan — a dry wind strongest from late November to mid-March. Together, these winds carry large amounts of dust and sand across the Mauritanian territory.

## Projected Climate Change

All four target hubs are projected to experience an increase in both minimum and maximum temperatures under future RCP4.5 (intermediate) and RCP8.5 (high) greenhouse gas emissions scenarios, with larger temperature increases projected under RCP8.5 than RCP4.5. These projected changes are reflected in Table 31, Annex 2. In line with increasing temperatures, the frequency of heat waves is predicted to increase across all four target hubs by 2050 under both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emissions scenarios.

Projected changes in the number of days with heatwaves per decade are presented in Table 42 in Annex 2.

## Projected Evolution of Precipitation Trends

The projected evolution of precipitation trends varies among hubs. For example, no significant change in precipitation is projected in Aoujeft by 2050 under the RCP4.5 GHG emissions scenario. In contrast, annual precipitation is expected to significantly decrease in Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema, relative to the reference period (1981–2010). Similarly, under the RCP8.5 emissions scenario, mean annual precipitation in Aoujeft is not projected to change significantly relative to the reference period. However, by 2050, mean annual precipitation is expected to decrease in Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema. Projected changes in precipitation within the four target hubs are presented in Table 35 in Annex 2.

## Potential Evapotranspiration (ETP)

In line with increasing hub-level temperature trends and decreasing precipitation trends, potential evapotranspiration (ETP) is projected to increase across all four hubs by 2050 under both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emissions scenarios (Table 40 in Annex 2). As a result of increased ETP and limited rainfall, the water balance deficit (P-ETP) in all four hubs is also projected to increase significantly (p<0.1) by 2050, as shown in Table 41, Annex 2.

## Drought Conditions

The combination of projected temperature rise, ETP and declining rainfall will increase the extent and severity of droughts at national and local levels. By 2040, the median number of consecutive dry days (CDD) for Mauritania is predicted to be between 219 and 230, compared to the historical value of 216 days in 1995. Moreover, by

# Problem statement

Climate change will continue to exacerbate the drivers of desertification in Mauritania, threatening the structural integrity of the fragile oases that underpin the natural resource-based livelihoods of rural communities. Drivers of desertification include changes in the spatial and temporal patterns of rainfall and increases in temperatures and ETP that, when combined, lead to:  
i) more frequent, severe and prolonged drought events¹⁶;  
ii) reduced water security;  
iii) more intense desertification processes; and  
iv) siltation of dams, boreholes, wells and rivers, which reduces the accessibility and availability of already strained water resources  
v) the drying up of wells commonly used by pastoralist herders.  

Of these impacts, the increasing frequency, severity and time span of droughts are particularly damaging, as they already contribute to vegetation loss, promoting dune advancement and accelerated sand encroachment in fragile oases (see Figure 140 in Annex 2).

Without urgent intervention, these processes will worsen in the future. The Sahara Desert is expected to spread south into the country’s most fertile lands in the Sahelian region. Rural communities in Mauritania have limited financial and coordination capacity to slow the rate of desertification along the Sahel-Saharan boundary and improve water security.

# Preferred adaptation solution

An ecosystem-based adaptation approach within the target hubs is the preferred option for responding to the impacts of climate changed-induced desertification within the four target hubs because it is cost-effective, easily implemented and has the potential to provide valuable ecosystem goods and services to climate-vulnerable communities dependent on the oases (for an example, see Figure 145 in Annex 2). This approach includes establishing 2,123 ha of green-grey dune fixation infrastructure and 120 km of protective fence line to facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem services related to dune stabilisation and the supply of natural resources.

To support the adaptive capacity of the communities in the target hubs, the proposed project will develop or strengthen sustainable agriculture-based livelihoods. By providing an alternative source of income to current extraction-based livelihoods, these livelihood options will improve the sustainability of green-grey infrastructure approaches by mitigating the risk of communities resorting to maladaptive coping mechanisms.

# Barriers to the preferred solution

## Barrier 1: Limited coordination and technical capacity to implement adaptation interventions at the community level

Implementing entities, as well as government and non-governmental stakeholders in Mauritania, have limited institutional capacities and incentive to coordinate efforts to address climate risks, which has restricted CCA efforts in the four target hubs. Government officials at national-, regional- and local levels, as well as staff from crucial sectors at the local level, have limited technical capacity to develop and implement adaptation initiatives. Local authorities and stakeholders at the forefront of managing climate change impacts, such as community leaders, often have inadequate training on incorporating climate change considerations into decision-making or designing and implementing climate-resilient natural resource management and land-use planning.

## Barrier 2: Limited financial capacity to invest in climate-resilient land and natural resources management at the community level

Most urban centres in Mauritania are characterised by predominantly informal trade, limited infrastructure and public-service coverage, high levels of self-employment and low human capital. These characteristics generally discourage private-sector investment in sustainable development and CCA¹⁷. Although the Government of the Islamic Republic of Mauritania (GoM) has introduced a number of regulatory and legislative reforms in an attempt to boost the private sector by 2025 — for example, through the establishment of the Higher Council for Investment in 2020 — several obstacles persist, impeding local and foreign investment in sustainable development. These obstacles include:  
i) difficulty in accessing microfinance for local business development;  
ii) limited market access within rural and semi-urban areas;  
iii) low levels of technical and professional training;  
iv) limited governance within the financial services sector; and  
v) limited private-sector organisation¹⁸.  

Consequently, past projects have been unable to mobilise substantial private-sector investment in CCA. For this reason, the proposed project will leverage public-sector funding to co-finance adaptation interventions.

## Barrier 3: Limited knowledge of the climate change impacts, risks, vulnerabilities and adaptation options across the four target hubs

Communities in rural areas — particularly farmers and pastoralists — are not well informed about current and expected climate change impacts, risks and vulnerabilities related to water availability, agricultural production and other land-use practices. Moreover, t he target communities have limited technical knowledge on adapt ing to these changes and increasing climate resilience. To date, attempts to stabilise dunes in the four target hubs have rarely been consistent, strategic or integrated into comprehensive plans. The NAP Knowledge Management Platform is still under development, as a result, national -level stakeholders seldom pass on information to regional or local levels. There is limited inter-sectoral collaboration and knowledge-sharing between ministries, departments and stakeholders.

## Barrier 4: Limited integration of climate change adaptation into land-use planning and natural-resource management strategies at the regional and local levels

Although urban plans developed for N ema and Rachid incorporate some measures for  climate-risk reduction, mainstreaming adaptation and mitigation strategies into plans at the regional and local levels has largely been overlooked. In the past, failure to recognise the importance of a participatory approach has resulted in weak coordination between climate change-related projects and associated activities within the target region19,20. While some basic water-management plans exist within the target region, pasture- and rangeland -management plans that consider climate change are notably absent in the target hubs21.

## Project strategy for overcoming barriers to adaptation and achieving a paradigm shift in Mauritania

The goal of the proposed project is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems in northern Mauritania against the impacts of climate change. This will be achieved by investing in land rehabilitation and sand -dune fixation, water resources management, climate-resilient agriculture and livelihood diversification in four rural-urban hubs: Aoujeft (in the Adrar wilayah), Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah), Tamcheket (in the Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and Nema (in the Hodh El Chargui wilayah). To ensure the success of this approach, the project will focus on two main outcomes:  

- Outcome 1. Increased implementation and upscaling of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures across the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania.;  
- Outcome 2. Communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania have increased livelihood and water security as a result of investment in EbA measures, water access, and sustainable land - and natural resources management practices.

### Output 1.1 will address all four of the identified barriers to adaptation by establishing coordination platforms to improve technical capacity, facilitate knowledge sharing on natural resource management and sustainable land-use planning and the implementation of EbA activities at the regional and commune level. By creating and disseminating knowledge on EbA, Output 1.2 will contribute to addressing barrier 1 and barrier 3.

Under Output 2.1, ~2,123 ha of green-grey infrastructure will be established across the four target hubs to facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem services related to dune stabilisation and the supply of natural resources. Co-developing commune-level rehabilitation plans will support the integration of climate change adaptation into land -use planning and natural -resource management strategies at the regional and local level. Investment in climate-resilient land management will contribute to overcoming barriers 2 and 3.

Under Output 2.2, community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) will be established. Commune -level water monitoring and regulation plans will be developed, and physical water management infrastructure and water access points will be established along historical transhumance routes. This will contribute to overcoming barriers 2, 3 and 4.

Output 2.3 will facilitate the adoption of climate -smart agricultural practices and sustainable diversified livelihoods for farmers and community members within the target communes. Farmers will be trained by MEDD and MDR extension officers and representatives from commune-level technical committees (

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 7

Thereafter — and training to nursery operators, to support land rehabilitation, dune fixation and horticulture activities. As some nurseries already exist within the project’s target regions, there is an opportunity for upscaling as well as the establishment of new nurseries and market gardens, encouraging livelihood diversification and empowering women in agriculture. In addition, an on-granting mechanism will promote and support community-led initiatives, fostering the autonomous uptake of EbA practices by community members, thereby increasing community ownership and the sustainability of project activities beyond the project implementation timeline.

The theory of change (ToC) states that If an ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach is used to restore ecosystems, fix dunes in place and sustainably manage natural resources along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania, supported by transformational change in policies, planning, knowledge management and capacity-building, then the resilience of communities and ecosystems to the impacts of climate change-induced desertification — including sand inundation, water scarcity, rangeland degradation and reduced agricultural productivity — will be strengthened because the provision of ecosystem services will be enhanced, communities in rural-urban hubs will have strengthened livelihood security and sub-national decision-makers will have strengthened capacities to invest in and implement EbA measures. This resilience will be enhanced particularly if supported by transformational change in policies, planning, knowledge management and capacity-building. As ecosystem services are enhanced, livelihood security for communities in rural-urban hubs will be enhanced. Moreover, sub-national decision-makers’ capacity to invest in and implement EbA measures will be strengthened. This, in turn, will support a paradigm shift towards effective CCA in Mauritania.

The assumptions underpinning the success of the proposed project are that: i) target communities and the government will continue to engage in the development and maintenance of adaptation interventions; ii) climate change knowledge generated will be used to inform effective management practices in the long term; iii) sustainable livelihoods will provide sufficient incentive to prevent the continuation of unsustainable land-management practices; and iv) improving market access will increase adoption of sustainable agriculture practices.

The selection of ARA1, ARA2, ARA3 and ARA4 is based on the direct and measurable contribution of the project outcomes and outputs to enhancing community and ecosystem resilience in the target hubs. The proposed project adopts an integrated ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach that addresses environmental and socio-economic vulnerabilities caused by desertification and climate change.

### Outcome 1
- **ARA1**: Under Outcome 1, the project will strengthen governance and technical capacity for climate adaptation by establishing local coordination structures and integrating climate change considerations into sub-national plans and budgets. The involvement of community representatives in these structures will enhance community ownership of adaptation measures, ensuring that vulnerable groups have a platform to influence decision-making and benefit directly from resilience-building interventions.
- **ARA4**: The strengthened governance and knowledge-sharing mechanisms established under Outcome 1 will improve the planning and management of ecosystem restoration activities. By mainstreaming ecosystem-based adaptation into local plans and enhancing coordination among stakeholders, the project will promote sustainable land-use practices and strengthen the long-term resilience of ecosystems.

### Outcome 2
- **ARA1**: Under Outcome 2, the project will improve the resilience of vulnerable communities by reducing exposure to desertification and sand encroachment through the establishment of green-grey infrastructure. Additionally, training and technical support for climate-resilient agriculture, sustainable livestock management and alternative livelihoods will enable communities to diversify income sources and reduce dependency on fragile ecosystems.
- **ARA2**: Under Outcome 2, the project will enhance water and food security by establishing water management infrastructure, installing improved irrigation systems and promoting sustainable water management practices. Increased access to water for agricultural and household use will improve community health and well-being while climate-resilient agricultural practices will strengthen food security.
- **ARA3**: Under Outcome 2, the project will increase the resilience of infrastructure by supporting the establishment of green-grey dune fixation infrastructure and protective greenbelts. These measures will protect houses, roads and other critical infrastructure from sand encroachment, thereby reducing long-term maintenance costs, increasing the longevity of built infrastructure and mitigating displacement resulting from sand encroachment on infrastructure.
- **ARA4**: Under Outcome 2, the project will restore and protect ecosystems by establishing green-grey dune fixation infrastructure and improving natural resource management. Increased ecosystem health will enhance the provision of ecosystem services such as soil stabilisation, groundwater recharge and improved biodiversity, strengthening the overall resilience of both ecosystems and communities.

This alignment demonstrates how the project’s integrated approach will generate social, economic and environmental adaptation benefits, directly supporting the GCF's strategic objectives. Further information regarding the proposed project’s contribution to the GCF results areas is provided in Section D.1.

# B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The objective of the proposed project is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems against climate change-induced desertification in the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania. The project will focus on four rural-urban hubs: Aoujeft (in the Adrar wilayah), Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah), Tamcheket (in the Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and Nema (in the Hodh El Chargui wilayah). For a more detailed description of the proposed project’s Outcome, Outputs and activities, please refer to Section 11 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

## Outcome 1. Increased implementation and upscaling of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures across the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania.

Outcome 1 will contribute to creating an enabling environment for climate change and gender-responsive land-use planning and natural resource management at the sub-national level in Mauritania. This will enhance the long-term sustainability and scalability of climate resilience-building interventions across the Sahara-Sahel boundary.

## Output 1.1 will strengthen governance structures to support the implementation, maintenance and upscaling of EbA measures across the four target hubs and the integration of climate change considerations and Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) into government plans, policies and budgets. This will be achieved by establishing and training commune-level technical committees (CTCs) in 12 priority communes across Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema (process for selection of priority communes is detailed in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study), to facilitate natural resource management, sustainable land-use planning, EbA implementation and knowledge sharing at regional and local levels. Each CTC will be chaired by the respective commune's mayor, with members comprising DREDD representatives; 1-2 members of the Communal Council, representatives of Associations for Natural Resources (AGLCs) and of Association of Oasis Producers’ Groups (AOPGs); civil society representatives; and local community members. The CTCs will be trained by extension officers from the MEDD and DREDDs to supervise, manage and upscale CCA measures introduced under Output 2.1, Output 2.2 and Output 2.3 of the proposed project. The CTCs will also coordinate knowledge-sharing between local community members, commune-level authorities (mayors) and regional authorities (DREDDs and walis).

## Output 1.2 will focus on developing and disseminating knowledge products that support informed decision-making for climate change adaptation and the upscaling of the project approach and best practices. Knowledge products generated under the project will include training manuals, implementation guides, lesson learnt and best practices identified by both experts and local stakeholders, and monitoring and evaluation reports. These knowledge products will be disseminated to stakeholders across the four wilayahs (including non-target communes) using the NAP Knowledge Management Platform, which is currently under development by the NAP readiness project. The project will also support the NAP Knowledge Management Platform to better reach local-level end users, by packaging knowledge in formats that are accessible to sub-national decision-makers and communities.

## Table 74 in Annex 2 shows how the project aligns with the GCF Ecosystem and Ecosystem Services Sectoral Guide (2022).

| Co-benefit 1.     | Improved health of target communities | ☒ | ☒ | ☐ | ☒ | ☒ | ☐ |
| Co-benefit 2.     | Enhanced biodiversity in the target communes | ☒ | ☒ | ☐ | ☒ | ☒ | ☐ |

| Co-benefit 4:     | Sustainable economic growth and development | ☒ | ☒ | ☒ | ☐ | ☐ | ☐ |
| Co-benefit 5:     | Improved food security | ☐ | ☒ | ☒ | ☒ | ☐ | ☐ |

## CTCs will provide a platform for regular engagement between stakeholders at all administrative levels. This will facilitate the two-way flow of information between representatives at the village, commune, moughataa, wilayah and national levels, as shown in Table 10.

### Output 1.1. Governance structures are strengthened to support the implementation of EbA measures and the integration of climate change considerations and EbA into government plans, policies and budgets.

## Activity 1.1.1. Establish coordination platforms and train participants to facilitate knowledge sharing, natural-resource management, sustainable land-use planning and the implementation of EbA activities at regional and local levels.

Under Activity 1.1.1, the proposed GCF project will:
- establish 12 commune-level technical committees (CTCs) across the four target hubs;
- enhance capacity for coordination and knowledge-sharing between local and regional stakeholders within the target hubs; and
- support the integration of climate change considerations and EbA into sub-national plans, policies and budgets.

To maximise the impact potential project activities, given the finite nature of financial resources and human capital 23, CTCs will be established within the 12 priority communes targeted by the project (i.e., most vulnerable and exposed), with the view to upscale project interventions to additional communes in the future.

### Table 1. The 12 priority communes for the proposed project

| Hub | Aoujeft | Rachid Tamcheket | Nema |
|-----|---------|------------------|------|
| Target communes | • Ain Savra | • El Medah | • Maeden |
| | • Aoujeft | • Tidjikja | • El Wahat |
| | • El Mabrouk | • Gueate Teidoume | • Oualata |
| | • N'Beiket Lahwach | • Noual | • Jreif |

Representatives of the CTCs will coordinate with DREDD directorates in each target wilayah to oversee the following mandates:
- enhance cross-sectoral coordination for CCA between national, regional and local stakeholders;
- ensure that target communities' needs and concerns are considered when developing and implementing natural-resource management and sustainable land-use plans and policies;
- facilitate two-way information- and knowledge-sharing between local-level stakeholders and regional authorities;
- support the training of community members to implement and maintain adaptation interventions introduced under proposed the project;
- support the integration of climate change considerations into regional- and commune-level policies, plans and budgets for natural-resources management and land-use planning; and
- develop the technical and institutional capacities of relevant stakeholders to drive scalable and sustainable climate action.

In accordance with Mauritanian law, the 12 CTCs will be formally established via announcement; however, over the course of the project, their legitimacy in the relevant legislature will be pursued via registration with the Ministry of the Interior. Formalisation of these project structures will maintain their medium- to long-term sustainability and facilitate community ownership of project activities beyond the project implementation period.

Each CTC will be chaired by the mayor of its respective commune and its members will include, inter alia, representatives from the regional environmental delegation (DREDD), 1-2 members of the Communal Council, civil society representatives, and community members. Existing community structures and systems — such as already established Associations for Natural Resources (AGLCs) — will be leveraged, where applicable, to accelerate the establishment of the CTCs and support their sustainability.

Sub-activities implemented to deliver this activity are:
- Sub-activity 1.1.1.1. Establish commune-level technical committees (CTCs) within each priority commune in the four target hubs.
- Sub-activity 1.1.1.2. Deliver training workshops to CTCs, enhancing members' capacities to implement and manage project activities, support the integration of climate change in regional- and commune-level policies, plans and budgets, and facilitate knowledge sharing between regional- and local-level stakeholders.

## Output 1.2. Knowledge products developed and disseminated to support decision making and upscaling.

### Activity 1.2.1. Develop knowledge products on project lessons learned and best practices through a participatory process engaging the communities.

To ensure that project interventions are responsive to local adaptation needs and generate lasting benefits, Activity 1.2.1 will focus on systematically capturing community perspectives, lessons learned and best practices that emerge throughout project implementation. By integrating participatory approaches, the project will facilitate knowledge exchange between local stakeholders and the Project Management Unit (PMU), enabling adaptive management and the dissemination of successful adaptation strategies that can be referenced when upscaling or replicating climate change adaptation strategies across Mauritania, or when developing new climate change adaptation projects.

Under this activity, bi-annual gender-inclusive discussions will be held between the PMU, the CTCs and communities in the target hubs. These discussions will provide a structured forum for community members to share their experiences, voice concerns and highlight successes and challenges related to project interventions. Ensuring gender inclusivity in these engagements will help capture diverse perspectives, particularly from marginalised groups that may otherwise be excluded from decision-making processes. The insights gained from these discussions will be synthesised into community engagement reports, which will serve as a record of local experiences and provide valuable feedback for refining project implementation strategies.

In parallel, the project will undertake a systematic identification of best practices and lessons learned emerging from project interventions. These knowledge products will be documented in lessons learned and best practice reports, as well as in regular M&E reports, capturing successful approaches, lessons learned and strategies that have proven effective in enhancing climate resilience at the community level. These reports will be made accessible to the public via the NAP knowledge management platform and other channels appropriate to the local context (Activity 1.2.2).

By generating and sharing knowledge products that reflect community experiences and successful adaptation practices, this activity will contribute to evidence-based decision-making and capacity building at local, national and regional levels.

#### Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 1.2.1 are:

- Sub-activity 1.2.1.1. Hold bi-annual gender-inclusive discussions between representatives from the PMU, CTCs and communities in the target hubs on project intervention successes and challenges, and develop these discussions into community engagement reports.
- Sub-activity 1.2.1.2. Identify lessons learned and best practices used in project interventions, and develop these into implementation guides and best practice reports.

### Activity 1.2.2. Enhance the dissemination of adaptation knowledge to sub-national decision-makers and communities to support upscaling.

To ensure that knowledge generated through the project is widely accessible and supports long-term climate change adaptation efforts, the proposed project will facilitate the dissemination of knowledge products through both national and international platforms. The primary mechanism for this will be the NAP knowledge management platform, which is currently under development in Mauritania, where monitoring reports, GCF evaluation reports, community engagement reports, records of the validation workshop, final policy briefs and best practices generated under Activity 1.2.1 will be uploaded and stored. This centralised repository will enhance access to critical adaptation information for policymakers, practitioners and community-level stakeholders.

Outcome 2. Communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania have increased livelihood and water security as a result of investment in EbA measures, water access, and sustainable land- and natural resources management practices.

Outcome 2 will enhance livelihood security for communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania by promoting investment in Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures and sustainable land and natural resource-management practices.

The project will stabilise dunes and reduce sand encroachment by utilising EbA dune-fixation measures (green infrastructure) within twelve priority communes across the Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema hubs. EbA dune stabilisation techniques will include: i) establishing community-managed buffer zones, or protective greenbelts, along priority communes’ borders; and ii) utilising both biological and mechanical dune-fixation techniques (described below) to stabilise dunes in vulnerable areas identified by target communities. These EbA measures will be supplemented by fencing (grey infrastructure), which will be installed around newly established dune-stabilisation sites to protect newly planted vegetation from livestock.

Climate-resilient livestock-management practices will include rotational grazing, supplementary feeding and 'livestock collar re-seeding'. By incorporating these climate-resilient livestock management practices, the project will enhance the resilience of pastoral livelihoods and reduce pressure on natural resources. This approach will complement the establishment of green-grey infrastructure to control sand encroachment and slow the rate of desertification.

Output 2.1. Green-grey dune fixation infrastructure is established to control sand encroachment, enhance the provision of ecosystem services and slow the rate of desertification within the four target hubs.

Activity 2.1.1. Establish 2,123 hectares of EbA dune-fixation infrastructure and 120 kilometres of protective fencing across the four target hubs, to facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem services related to dune stabilisation and the supply of natural resources.

Under this activity, ~2,123 hectares (ha) of EbA dune-fixation measures will be implemented across the four target hubs. These measures will include:

- ~1,138 ha of protective green belts (buffer zones); and
- ~985 ha of mechanical and biological dune-fixation infrastructure.

Green belts in strategic locations will slow sand encroachment and subsequent desertification under future climate change conditions, with natural vegetation serving as a barrier to approaching sand dunes (as shown in Figure 142 in Annex 2). This will involve restoring degraded ecosystems and actively planting protective tree lines along the boundaries of priority communes, to create buffer zones between vulnerable communities and mobile sand dunes (for an example of protected tree lines, see Figure 143 in Annex 2). Each green belt will be ~500 m wide, with restored ecosystems and planted treelines forming a continuous barrier against southward-shifting sand dunes. The strategic positioning of these buffer zones along the north-eastern boundaries of Aoujeft, Rachid and Tamcheket will, in turn, shield south-western communes against the impacts of sand encroachment in the long-term. Using GIS software, the number of hectares of EbA dune-fixation measures needed to establish proposed buffer zones in each hub was calculated (Table 2).

### Table 2. Total area of EbA dune fixation measures needed to establish proposed buffer zones in each target hub.

| Hub        | Area of proposed buffer zone (ha) |
|------------|-----------------------------------|
| Aoujeft    | 325                               |
| Rachid     | 296                               |
| Tamcheket  | 333                               |
| Nema       | 185                               |
| Total      | 1,139                             |

Mechanical dune-fixation will involve the construction of windbreak hedges via wattling (Figure 145 in Annex 2), to form 'stop dunes', or 'check dunes' (see Figure 146 in Annex 2). These structures form a physical barrier between advancing sand dunes and target communities, while the checkerboard pattern assists with capturing sand as it is carried by prevailing winds.

Green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure will be installed along the perimeter of each vulnerable town, to ensure that all critical infrastructure is loosely enclosed by treelines, wattling and check dunes. Each of the five towns in Figure 147 in Annex 2 has an area of ~74 ha (740,000 m²); therefore, a total of ~370 ha of green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure (fences, wattling and check dunes) are needed to protect critical infrastructure against sand encroachment at these sites.

Community members in the target hubs of Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema identified several important sites requiring biological and mechanical dune-fixation during stakeholder consultations - outlined in Table 3 below. Details regarding why these sites were selected are provided in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

The total area of mechanical- and biological dune fixation infrastructure required to protect vulnerable sites identified via GIS software and stakeholder consultation is equivalent to 985 ha.

### Table 3. Number of hectares (ha) of green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure required to protect each of the vulnerable sites identified by stakeholders within the four target hubs.

| Hub        | Site Area of green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure (ha) | Proposed dimensions (m) | Perimeter (m) |
|------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------|---------------|
| Aoujeft    | Toueïguidit                                               | 2,500 x 500             | 6,000         |
|            | Ain Savra                                                 | 7,000 x 100             | 14,200        |
| Rachid     | The road on the Hssey Lgara–Touajil axis                  | 35,000 x 100            | 70,200        |
| Tamcheket  | Tamcheket town                                            | 2,000 x 100             | 4,200         |
| Nema       | City of Noual                                             | 5,000 x 100             | 10,200        |
| Total      |                                                           |                         | 104,800       |

Fencing will be installed around green-grey dune-fixation sites, to protect vegetation against foraging and trampling by free-roaming animals. Accordingly, fences will be erected around each of the dune-fixation sites identified in Table 3 above.

The planting of treelines for biological dune fixation in buffer zones will follow the best practices described in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study and all tree seedlings used during planting exercises will be sourced from local nurseries established under Activity 2.2.1, to support livelihood diversification efforts undertaken by the project.

Treelines will comprise a mixture of fast- and slow-growing, climate-resilient species, in accordance with the best practices and lesson learned outlined in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

Regional livestock cooperatives and breeders' associations exist within all of the proposed project's target wilayah; therefore, the proposed project will collaborate with representatives from these organisations — via the commune-level CTCs — to facilitate the adoption of climate-resilient livestock-management practices. Measures will include replacing local livestock breeds with climate-resilient breeds, growing climate-resilient fodder varieties for supplementary feeding during the lean season 25, and establishing 'set-aside' rotational grazing systems to rehabilitate degraded ecosystems and rangelands.

Additionally, pastoral water points and livestock corridors will be established to reverse sedentarisation trends among historically transhumant pastoralist communities, reducing anthropogenic pressure on natural ecosystems

## Output 2.2. Improved access to water for land rehabilitation and agricultural activities.

### Activity 2.2.1. Establish community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) and commune-level water monitoring and regulation plans.

Commune-level water user groups (WUGs) will be formed in the 12 priority communes. Each WUG will be led by elected community leaders from the respective priority communes and will report to the CTCs. WUG members will comprise local community members and representatives from livestock cooperatives, farmer-based organisations and women's groups in each target commune. WUGs will be trained to monitor water availability and usage within their respective communes and to oversee and manage the construction, operation and maintenance of infrastructure for capturing surface water, managing runoff flow, increasing groundwater recharge and reducing flood risks.

A team of national experts — comprising two hydrogeologists from the Ministry of Water and Sanitation (MHA 26) and two meteorologists from the Meteorological Service of Mauritania (ONM) — will conduct hydrogeological studies to quantify and map groundwater and surface water resources within each of the target hubs. In particular, this team will be mandated to identify flood-risk areas and determine the interconnectivity of aquifers, informing the selection of strategic sites to install: i) groundwater dams, for improving aquifer recharge; ii) gabions, dikes and stone bunds for reducing flood risks; and iii) reservoirs, boreholes, well points and rainwater-harvesting systems to increase rainwater capture and storage. The selection of target sites for water management infrastructure will be summarised in the form of commune-level water management plans.

#### Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.2.1. are:

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.1.** Establish community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) within each target commune and train members to implement and maintain water-related activities introduced under Activity 2.2.2 of the project.
- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.2.** Support CTCs to raise awareness about sustainable water usage and co-develop commune-level water monitoring and regulation plans in collaboration with WUGs.
- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.3.** Conduct hydrogeological studies (or consult existing hydrogeological maps, where applicable) and engage with WUG members to identify priority sites to install water-management infrastructure (Sub-activities 2.2.2.1, 2.2.2.2 and 2.2.3.1) in each target commune. Summarise the finding of site selection into a commune-level water management plan for each priority commune.

### Activity 2.2.2. Install physical water management infrastructure — including weirs, gabions, dikes, stone- and earthen bunds, groundwater dams and water access points for pastoralists.

Under Activity 2.2.2, water security within the proposed project’s target hubs will be strengthened by installing flood management infrastructure — including weirs, gabions, dikes, stone bunds, earthen bunds and groundwater dams — in each priority commune. These measures will reduce surface water runoff, increase groundwater infiltration and recharge rates, and enhance the capture of rainfall for use in supporting sustainable agriculture and climate-resilient livelihoods.

Additionally, pastoral water access points — that is, boreholes equipped with solar pumps — will be established in graras and along historical transhumance routes, to support year-round agricultural production and transhumance and reverse sedentarisation trends among nomadic pastoralists. ## Activity 2.2.3. Install 12 rainwater-harvesting systems and communal cisterns (5,000 L per system) within each target commune

Under Activity 2.2.3 of the proposed project, the installation of rainwater harvesting (RWH) systems and communal cisterns will increase the capture of surface water runoff during extreme rainfall events and increase target communities’ capacities to store water for use during prolonged periods of drought. To promote fair access, sites located near communal buildings (town halls, schools and health posts) and agricultural areas (including market gardens and nurseries) will be given preference, to ensure water is used for agricultural activities and sustainable livelihoods introduced under the proposed project.

To establish the RWH systems and communal cisterns, local labour will be employed and supervised by trained WUG members. Twelve RWH systems and communal cisterns, with a capacity of 5,000 litres each, will be distributed to each of the 12 priority communes, totalling 144 RWH systems and cisterns across the four target hubs. Details regarding site selection for these interventions are provided in Section 6 and 7 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

## Output 2.3. Climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods based on sustainable land- and natural resource-use are developed and/or strengthened to reduce land degradation and support climate-resilient income-generation by community members within the target regions.

## Activity 2.3.1. Facilitate the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices and sustainable, diversified livelihoods by farmers within the target communes

The project will facilitate the adoption of climate-resilient practices (Activity 2.3.1). The project will encourage livelihood diversification by supporting the uptake of sustainable income-generating activities such as beekeeping, poultry farming and value-added processing, reducing dependence on traditional agricultural activities and building resilience against climate-related risks. To further improve rural farmers’ resilience to the impacts of climate-change desertification, MEDD and the Ministry of Rural Development (MDR) extension officers will supply local nurseries with an initial stock of climate-resilient seed varietals for sale and distribution to local farmers and market gardeners within each of the target hubs and train nursery operators to maintain sustainable stocks (or seed banks).

Horticultural centres established in each priority commune will be supplied with the initial materials needed to commence business operations. These may include beehives for apiculture, climate-resilient seed varietals for seed banks and irrigation kits for market gardens. Access to markets to sell horticultural produce will be improved by: i) organising weekly markets in each priority commune — arranged by CTCs — to facilitate trade between local community members; and ii) using ‘community carts’ to transport goods to larger, urban markets within the target hubs and surrounding areas. Please refer to Table 82 in Annex 2 for a summary of the types of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices currently implemented and Table 84 in Annex 2 for a summary of potential alternative livelihoods.

## Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.3.1 are:

- **Sub-activity 2.3.1.1.** Establish nurseries and seed banks in each target commune to supply activities related to land rehabilitation and dune fixation (Activity 2.1.1), CSA practices (Sub-activity 2.3.1.3) and horticultural activities such as market-gardening (Sub-activity 2.3.1.4).

- **Sub-activity 2.3.1.2.** Collect cuttings and seeds from agricultural crop species, as well as indigenous grass and tree species, to serve as stock material for nurseries and seed banks established under Sub-activity 2.3.1.1.

- **Sub-activity 2.3.1.3.** Train farmers within the target communes to practice climate-resilient crop agriculture and use improved agricultural technologies, including drip irrigation kits, solar powered pumps, integrated pest management strategies, zai pits and half-moons.

- **Sub-activity 2.3.1.4.** Conduct site visits and provide technical support to facilitate the uptake of sustainable livelihood activities — including horticulture (market-gardening), apiculture, poultry farming, livestock feed production and the collection and sale of non-timber forest products — by community members within the target communes.

- **Sub-activity 2.3.1.5.** Supply farmers and horticulturalists with water-efficient irrigation equipment and climate-resilient crop varieties to support the uptake of agricultural activities adopted under Sub-activities 2.3.1.3 and 2.3.1.4.

- **Sub-activity 2.3.1.6.** Improve access to urban markets and develop value chains for offloading agricultural produce within each target commune, to enhance income generated from sustainable agricultural livelihoods.

## Activity 2.3.2. Establish a small grants facility to facilitate continued investment in upscaling successful EbA activities and sustainable livelihoods.

A project Small Grants Facility (SGF) will be established to enhance local communities’ access to finance to upscale successful EbA interventions and sustainable, agriculture-based livelihoods introduced under the project. The on-granting mechanism will promote and support community-led initiatives, fostering the autonomous uptake of EbA practices by community members. GCF Proceeds will be used to finance the sub-grants. The SGF will be housed in MEDD, and sub-grant agreements will be executed between MEDD and each sub-grant recipient.

An SGF Steering Committee will be instituted to guide and oversee the establishment and operations of the SGF. It will be chaired by UNEP as the project AE, and will include representation from MEDD and other relevant Ministries (the Ministry of Economy and Finance; the Ministry of Agriculture; the Ministry of Livestock Farming; the Ministry of Housing, Urbanism and Regional Planning), as well as from UNDP (to ensure links and synergies with the Small Grants Programme it manages). The SGF Steering Committee will review the recommended funding proposals and jointly make the grant award decisions. UNEP as the chair of the SGF Steering Committee will report on its operations to the PSC.

Table 81 in Annex 2 outlines the preliminary proposed design of the SGF, and the process for submitting and screening sub-grant proposals. The screening begins with CTCs (including representatives of vulnerable groups and CSOs) and is completed by the SGF team housed in MEDD (see below), before submission to the SGF Steering Committee.

Establishing and operating a project SGF requires procuring an SGF management team to be housed in MEDD, to: i) oversee the facility’s operation and maintenance; and ii) manage the approval, disbursement and monitoring of small grants throughout the project implementation period. Prior to the SGF establishment, an international consultant (finance expert) will develop the SGF’s policies, frameworks and guiding principles. These will be built on lessons learned and best practice from other projects in the region. For example, the experience with the project Enhancing resilience of communities to the adverse effects of climate change on food security in Mauritania (PARSACC) showed that building social and capital was a key success factor.

The international consultant will also design a funding proposal template and accompanying selection criteria for the SGF, streamlining the small-grants approval process. Capacity building support will be provided to MEDD to strengthen MEDD’s institutional, operational and financial capacity for the design, execution and oversight of the SGF. This consultancy will also provide technical backstopping to MEDD on these aspects throughout the implementation of the SGF.

# B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

## Governance structures

The implementation arrangements and governance structure for the proposed project are illustrated in Figure 10.

### Figure 10. Project implementation arrangements and governance structures

Accredited Entity  
UNEP is the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project, as designated by the Government of the Islamic Republic of Mauritania (GoM). The AE will be responsible for the overall management of the project as well as for the financial monitoring and reporting aspects of the proposed project's activities. UNEP will be responsible for overseeing the implementation, monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of the project in coordination with the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and the Project Management Unit (PMU). A UNEP Programme Officer (PO) will be responsible for project supervision to ensure compliance with GCF's and UNEP's standard policies and operating procedures. The PO will be required to participate in annual Project Steering Committee (PSC) meetings. The PO will be supported by a UNEP Fund Management Officer (FMO) on financial monitoring and supervision.

## Experience and track record of AE

UNEP has a broad global portfolio on climate change projects funded via the, inter alia, GCF, Least Developed Countries Fund (LDCF), Special Climate Change Fund (SCCF), Adaptation Fund (AF) and bilateral funds such as the European Commission. From these projects, UNEP has supported national governments and local communities to adapt to climate change. UNEP's knowledge base is derived from completed and ongoing projects supporting:  
i) methods and tools for decision-making;  
ii) prioritising, designing and implementing adaptation interventions;  
iii) enhancing climate resilience by restoring vulnerable ecosystems that underpin community livelihoods; and  
iv) monitoring the socioeconomic and environmental benefits of adaptation interventions.

UNEP also has a longstanding relationship in Mauritania, having supported the country in GEF enabling activities, including the development of the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) as well as national communications since 1997. UNEP is also currently supporting the GCF National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Readiness Programme and has assisted the implementation of several CCA projects in Mauritania with

The support of international climate change funds, including the GEF -funded projects, EbA South and DIMS. As the GCF AE, UNEP will draw on previous experience and lessons learned to oversee the efficient and effective delivery of the project objectives during the implementation of the proposed project.

## Executing Entity

The proposed project will be implemented over a six-year period from 2025 to 2030 and executed by Mauritania's Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD). The MEDD is tasked with developing and executing the National Sustainable Development Plan (SNEDD), implementing the National Environmental Action Plan (PANE II), formulating operational plans, and overseeing and securing financial and material support for program implementation. It has created sector-specific plans and programmes concerning desertification and climate change, biodiversity, wetland conservation, and coastal ordinance. These areas of expertise and context-specific knowledge allow the MEDD to provide the necessary oversight and guidance during the implementation of the project. The MEDD also serves as the country's focal point for the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). Moreover, the Coordination Cell for the National Climate Change Programme (Cellule de Coordination du Programme National sur le Changement Climatique, CCPNCC) operates within the MEDD to coordinate and implement climate change initiatives in the country, as well as handle reporting, such as the National Communications III. Both the MEDD and CCPNCC consult directly with several ministries and departments to promote these programs.

As the Executing Entity (EE), the MEDD will coordinate the implementation of the project at national level and will report to UNEP as the AE. To ensure that the project remains in line with national policies, MEDD and UNEP will establish a Project Steering Committee (discussed below).

## Project Steering Committee

To ensure project compliance with national policies, MEDD and UNEP will establish a Project Steering Committee (PSC). This committee will be composed of at least one representative from the MEDD, the Ministry of Agriculture; the Ministry of Livestock Farming; the Ministry of Water and Sanitation; and the Ministry of Housing, Urbanism and Regional Planning, respectively. In addition, to ensure local representation, a representative of regional councils (rotating between project regions), a mayor representing communes (rotating between communes) and a CSO representative will be included in the PSC. A representative of UNEP will also form part of the PSC. This committee will have a decision-making capacity and will primarily serve to provide project oversight and advisory support, including: a) overseeing project implementation; and b) reviewing annual budget and workplans. The committee will meet at least twice a year (once per semester), with ad hoc meetings as required to discuss project results and performance indicators and provide strategic guidance.

## Technical Committee

In addition to the PSC, a Technical Committee will be established under the chairmanship of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) to provide technical guidance and ensure ownership, communication and reporting on the project with national adaptation communities. This Technical Committee will be composed of one representative from the Ministry of Economic Affairs, Development and Finance, the Ministry of Interior and Decentralization, the Ministry of Transportation and Infrastructure , the Ministry of Petroleum, Energy and Mines, the Ministry of Social Affairs, Children and Family, the Ministry of Higher Education and Scientific Research, and the National Bureau of Statistics, respectively. The Technical Committee will also require specific technical support from other relevant stakeholders , as required . These stakeholders will include tertiary education institutions in Mauritania, notably the Nouakchott University El Asriya (UNEA), Ecole Normale Supérieure (ENS), Scientific Institute for Technology Education (ISET) or the Mauritanian Institute for Scientific Research (IMRS). The Technical Committee will seek cooperation opportunities with potential project partners including, inter alia, UNDP Country Office, UNOPS, IFAD, FAO, World Bank, AfDB, AFD, GIZ, CIDA and IUCN, as well as national NGOs and private sector associations. The Technical Committee will report to the PSC and will meet at least twice a year (once per semester), with ad hoc meetings held when required.

## Project Management Unit

The PMU will be embedded within the MEDD and will consist of: i) a Project Coordinator (PC); ii) a Financial and Administrative Officer; iii) a Procurement Officer; iv) a Chief Technical Advisor (CTA); v) a Communications Officer; vi) a Gender Officer; vii) an Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Officer; and viii) a Monitoring and Evaluation Officer.

### Mission and objectives

The organisation is dedicated to supporting state policies focused on environmental protection and poverty alleviation using participatory approaches that involve all stakeholders. Its goals include:

- Participating in national, sub-regional or international actions promoting childhood support and indigent care.

Naforé is actively engaged in conservation efforts that address challenges related to biodiversity, species protection, habitat protection, natural resource conservation, climate change mitigation, waste management, pollution control, sustainable agriculture and rural hygiene and sanitation. These initiatives reflect a comprehensive approach to environmental stewardship, with the aim of maintaining biodiversity, supporting sustainable land and natural resource use and enhancing community resilience to climate change and its effects.

### Project execution

Naforé has a proven track record of successfully managing projects funded by several national and international bodies, including inter alia UNDP, IUCN, UNICEF, FAO and GIZ. Projects such as promoting green jobs for rural youth, environmental awareness campaigns and improving hygiene and sanitation in healthcare centres demonstrate Naforé's ability to effectively utilise funds and achieve project goals.

### Strategic partnerships

The organisation has built strong partnerships with state agencies, international institutions and local communities, showcasing its collaborative approach and ability to work within the broader framework of Mauritania’s development policies.

### Expertise in environmental impact studies

Naforé has been involved in significant environmental impact studies for several sectors, including oil exploration and infrastructure development. These activities highlight its expertise in assessing and mitigating environmental impacts in line with national and international standards.

# B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Grant finance from the GCF is the most appropriate form of concessional grant finance for the proposed project. This is because there are limited financial resources for investment in climate-resilient sustainable development in Mauritania, which is a considerable barrier to the implementation of CCA interventions in the country. In addition, there is a high level of public and external debt in Mauritania, which limits the capacity of the GoM to repay loans. Moreover, the country is a least developed country (LDC), with low income and severe structural impediments to sustainable development. As a result, communities in Mauritania require support from the GCF to achieve a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient sustainable development.

Communities living along the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, particularly the increasing rates of desertification, dune advancement and water scarcity. In 2019, Mauritania was ranked the 20th most vulnerable country to climate change and other global challenges.

# B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed interventions aim to improve subsistence-level agriculture, water access, and health outcomes in marginalized communities. Grant funding is proposed as much of the value generated accrues to households through local consumption instead of through formal value chains and trade, which would generate the sufficient cashflows needed to sustain alternative funding instruments.

In addition, the high levels of interdimensional poverty in Mauritania; the high debt-to-GDP ratio of the GoM; and the low levels of economic development in the country add to the financial-level risk in the project. Furthermore, there is limited incentive for the private sector to participate in the proposed project because: i) there is limited presence of private sector entities in the target areas; and ii) the project has a low potential for cost-recovery. As a result, the private sector is excluded as an option for alternative funding for the proposed project.

The exit strategy for this project involves gradually transitioning from grant-based support to more sustainable financing mechanisms. This includes building local capacity for climate adaptation and mitigation, strengthening institutional frameworks, and promoting private sector engagement where feasible. The goal is to ensure that communities can maintain their adaptive capacity without relying solely on external assistance.

To achieve this, the project will focus on enhancing local ownership and participation in climate action initiatives. This involves training community members in sustainable practices, improving access to information and resources, and fostering partnerships between local organizations and development partners. Additionally, efforts will be made to integrate climate resilience into national policies and programs, ensuring long-term sustainability.

The proposed outcomes, outputs, and activities of this project have been designed to be fully aligned with the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF), with technical support provided by UNEP. The exit strategy aims to create a self-sufficient model that reduces dependency on external funding while maintaining the effectiveness of climate adaptation measures.

Overall, the exit strategy emphasizes the importance of building local capacity and promoting sustainable development practices. It seeks to ensure that communities can continue to adapt to climate change impacts without relying on continuous external support, thereby achieving long-term resilience and sustainability.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 23

## Sustainability through technical and institutional capacity building

The project will create a strengthened enabling environment that will support the long-term sustainability and scaling of CCA measures in Mauritania. This enabling environment will include:  
i) improved vertical (across national, regional and local levels) and horizontal (between government departments at the hub level) coordination mechanisms, including the commune-level technical committees (CTCs);  
ii) updated policies, plans and strategies that mainstream CCA and natural resource management in a gender-responsive manner;  
iii) strengthened knowledge management systems aimed at collecting, tailoring and disseminating CCA best practices.  

This will empower relevant government institutions at the national and sub-national levels to dedicate more resources and budget to incorporating gender-responsive CCA initiatives into development plans, policies and budgets.

Equipment provided through the project will also provide recipients (GoM, CTCs, cooperatives, associations etc.) with the technical capacity to continue implementing CCA measures. To ensure that this remains functional, the GoM will assume responsibility for the continued operation and maintenance of this equipment after the project lifespan. A structured plan, with responsible entities, estimated costs and funding sources will be developed as part of the project’s exit strategy.  

In cases where the equipment is provided to community user groups (e.g. water user groups or natural resource management associations), a management system will be established, with a fee structure that generates funds for the operation, maintenance and repair of the equipment. Where needed, maintenance costs will be integrated into government (MEDD) budgets, with a set schedule for inspections to identify maintenance needs. This will promote the sustainability of the CCA measures.

## Social and environmental sustainability

The geographical isolation of the target hubs means that community participation and ownership of the proposed project’s on-the-ground interventions are critical to their longevity during and past the project’s lifespan. Government stakeholders have highlighted this participatory approach as a critical component to ensure the success and longevity of project interventions. Community and decision-makers participation will be secured through the CTCs, WUGs and other community level groups.

## Financial sustainability

Several financing opportunities at the government and community levels will enable the continued O&M of interventions beyond the project’s lifespan. At the government level, the prioritisation of CCA, gender considerations and natural resource management into policies, plans and strategies will promote the allocation of sufficient funds to CCA initiatives through national, regional and local government budgets.

A critical component of achieving financial sustainability will be establishment of an on-granting mechanism under Activity 2.3.2, designed to provide financial stability and facilitate continued investment in upscaling successful EbA activities and sustainable livelihoods. To ensure the longevity and effectiveness of this mechanism beyond the project lifespan, the on-granting mechanism will be housed in MEDD, which has the mandate and capacity to manage and scale CCA and EbA initiatives. This entity will be responsible for overseeing the on-granting mechanism, ensuring that it continues to support community-led initiatives and foster the autonomous adoption and upscaling of successful EbA measures and sustainable agricultural-based livelihoods within the target hubs.

To solidify this transition, the project will work closely with the designated government entity to build its capacity in fund management, monitoring and evaluation, as well as in the strategic allocation of resources towards high-impact EbA interventions. This collaboration will include the development of clear guidelines for fund disbursement, criteria for project selection, and mechanisms for tracking and reporting on the impact of funded projects.

The on-granting mechanism will be embedded within an existing governmental framework to encourage directing a percentage of regional government development funds into the SGF. The long-term viability of this mechanism will be supported by the development of a monitoring and reporting system that will ensure the traceability and risk management of funds between the SGF and local-level stakeholders.

At the household level, the financial benefits incurred through improved:  
i) climate-resilient agricultural and alternative livelihoods;  
ii) access to water, food and wood fuel;  
iii) protection from permanent losses; and  
iv) built and natural capital through dune stabilisation are expected to incentivise individuals to maintain introduced practices, equipment and infrastructure after the project has concluded.

In total, the project’s economic benefit to cost ratio is estimated at 5.86 to 1 (Annex 3: FEA and Annex 3b). Additionally, technical support will be given to communities to facilitate market access for alternative livelihoods introduced by the project, which will assist in ensuring the long-term viability of these livelihoods.
