# C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

## C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities? Yes ☒    No ☐

## C.3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer? Yes ☒    No ☐

Technology transfer  
Outcome 1 of the proposed project will create a conducive environment for climate change and gender-responsive land-use planning and natural resource management in Mauritania, facilitating technology transfer through several key strategies. First, by strengthening the institutional capacity of decision-making bodies, Outcome 1 will enable the adoption and implementation of new technologies by national- and sub-national decision-makers. Knowledge sharing and dissemination are promoted under Outcome 1, by training stakeholders to leverage the NAP Knowledge Management Platform to facilitate the exchange of information and best practices. Overall, this outcome promotes a supportive environment that encourages capacity building, knowledge sharing, financial support, collaboration and long-term sustainability across all four target hubs, enabling beneficiaries to adopt and scale up innovative technologies to address climate change challenges and improve natural resource management in Mauritania.

Outcome 2 of the proposed project will facilitate technology transfer by implementing EbA interventions to restore the provisioning of ecosystem services towards water infiltration and control of sand encroachment in the four target hubs. These interventions will include establishing ~2,123 ha of green-grey dune-stabilisation infrastructure — using ecosystem restoration and biological- and mechanical dune fixation measures — at strategic sites within twelve priority communes across the four rural hubs. The installation of this infrastructure will contribute to ~65,700 individuals in the target hubs experiencing improved protection from the increasing impacts of sand inundation. Approximately 120 km of fencing will be established around dune-fixation sites, to ensure the protection of these sites and the ecosystem services accrued within them in the long-term.

The resilience of target communities to the impacts of drought will also be strengthened via the installation of physical structures such as gabions, dikes, boreholes, solar pumps, groundwater dams for improved aquifer recharge and rainwater harvesting systems. These structures will enhance the water security of more than 85,000 individuals across the target hubs by increasing groundwater storage and reducing overland water flow during heavy rainfall events, thereby limiting the amount of water lost through evaporation.

## Capacity-building interventions  
The engagement of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) as the Executing Entity for the project will contribute significantly to building the government’s technical, operational and project management capacity. In addition to supporting MEDD in addressing any procurement, human resource and/or financial management capacity gaps identified in the HACT micro-assessment and procurement assessment, the project interventions include specific capacity building support to strengthen MEDD’s institutional, operational and financial capacity for the design, execution and oversight of the Small Grants Facility.

Capacity-building activities under Outcome 1 include strengthening the legal and institutional framework of Mauritania for sustainable natural resource management and strategic land-use planning, to build the resilience of the rural Mauritanian population against the impacts of climate change-induced drought and desertification. Overall, the project will create a strengthened enabling environment that will support the long-term sustainability and scaling of climate change adaptation measures in Mauritania. This enabling environment will include: i) improved vertical and horizontal coordination mechanisms; ii) updated policies, plans and strategies that mainstream climate change adaptation and natural resource management; and iii) strengthened knowledge management systems aimed at collecting, tailoring and disseminating best practices. This will empower relevant government institutions at the national and sub-national levels to dedicate more resources and budget to incorporating gender-responsive climate change adaptation initiatives into development plans, policies and budgets.

Capacity-building initiatives implemented under Outcome 2 largely involve training community members on how to design, implement, manage EbA dune-stabilisation infrastructure, water resources management infrastructure and CSA interventions. The establishment and training of Water User Groups, for example, will strengthen the communities’ capacity to monitor water availability and usage, and to oversee and manage the construction, operation and maintenance of water infrastructure. Additionally, capacity-building under Outcome 2 includes training local community members on how to implement and manage climate-smart agriculture (CSA) interventions, including the use of drought-resistant crop varieties, improved irrigation techniques, and sustainable land management practices. These activities aim to enhance the adaptive capacity of communities to climate change impacts while promoting sustainable resource use.

# D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Direct beneficiaries

Defined as the ~85,229 people (40,877 males and 44,352 females) living within the twelve priority communes across the four target hubs, who will directly experience improved protection against sand inundation, enhanced access to markets and income-generating opportunities, and increased access to clean water via communal water points. The total number of direct beneficiaries represents ~1.8% of Mauritania’s total population and ~7% of the population in the four target wilayahs. Please refer to Table 1 in Annex 23 for a detailed breakdown of direct beneficiaries.

## Indirect beneficiaries

Those who benefit from the project outcomes indirectly through improved environmental conditions, policy changes or enhanced institutional capacities. This project estimates ~145,008 indirect beneficiaries (66,519 males and 78,489 females), representing individuals in surrounding or non-priority communes who will experience knowledge sharing benefits, improved coordination at national and regional levels and access to the small grants facility for scaling climate change adaptation interventions. This estimate represents ~3% of the country's total population and ~12% of the population in all four target wilayahs. Please refer to Table 2 in Annex 23 for a detailed breakdown of indirect beneficiaries.

## Contribution to GCF Results Areas

The proposed project will contribute to three GCF climate change resilience results areas, namely:  
i) ARA1 — increased resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities;  
ii) ARA2 — increased health, well-being, food and water security; and  
iii) ARA4 — increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services. Specific contributions to these result areas are described below.

### ARA1 — Increased resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities

Under Output 2.1, all individuals in the target hubs will gain enhanced protection from the escalating impacts of sand inundation. This will be achieved through enhanced ecosystem services generated by EbA and measures for physical dune fixation, including natural ecosystem restoration and conservation. The ecosystem services include dune fixation, improved soil stability and reduced airborne dust by establishing natural windbreaks. EbA solutions will be complemented by installing physical structures (fencing) around dune fixation sites established under Output 2.1.

Approximately 240 local community members will be given training on installing and maintaining these nature-based and physical structures, fostering resilience through ownership of these solutions.

Output 2.2 includes several activities that will increase community livelihood resilience to the impacts of climate change while reducing the anthropogenic landscape degradation, which increases ecosystems' and communities' exposure to desertification. Under this output:  
- 500 individuals will benefit from training on climate-resilient and sustainable livestock-management practices;  
- 500 individuals will receive training and inputs (such as equipment and crop seeds) for climate-resilient crop agriculture;  
- 500 individuals will be given technical support in adopting alternative, natural resource-based livelihoods.

### ARA2 — Increased health, well-being, food and water security

Under Activity 2.2.2, target communities' resilience to the impacts of drought will be enhanced through the installation of physical structures such as gabions, percolation tanks and injection wells, which increase aquifer recharge. These structures will enhance water security for ~85,000 people across the target hubs by increasing groundwater storage and reducing overland water flow during heavy rainfall events, limiting the amount of water lost through evaporation. Additionally, surface water flow and access for community members and nomadic pastoralists will be managed through

# D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The interventions contribute to four paradigm-shifting pathways, as identified in the GCF sectoral guidance, aimed at enhancing the climate resilience of ecosystems, and the agricultural and water sectors. These pathways include: i) transformational planning and programming; ii) catalysing climate innovation; iii) mobilising finance at scale; and iv) establishing coalitions and enhancing knowledge to scale up successful outcomes. Please refer to Table 75 in Annex 2 for a description of each pathway.

Across the four target wilayah covered by the project, the number of communes falling outside of the rural-urban hubs is as follows: 19 communes in Hodh El Chargui (outside of Nema hub); 22 communes in Hodh El Gharbi (outside of Tamcheket hub); 7 communes in Tagant (outside of Rachid hub); and lastly, 5 communes in Adrar wilayah (outside of Aoujeft hub). Collectively, these communes, which are not covered by the project target region, house ~802,600 individuals⁴⁰. Additionally, in the future, successful project interventions could be replicated in all 8 remaining wilayah in Mauritania, which collectively house a population of ~2.7 million⁴¹.

The mechanisms used for intra-wilayah scaling are equally applicable in scaling up project interventions beyond the four target wilayah. Spatial scaling will be achieved through mainstreaming climate change and gender considerations into regional plans, policies and budgets, with support from the DREDDs. Once formally recognised by the Ministry of the Interior, CTCs can be established in all remaining communes, to further strengthen coordination and knowledge-sharing, both within and across administrative levels. Newly established CTCs will then use lessons learned and best practices drawn from the proposed project — access via the NAP Knowledge Management Platform — to implement and manage successful CCA interventions in communes not covered by the target region. Please refer to Table 51 in Annex 2 for a detailed list of lessons learned.

Spatial upscaling of ecosystem rehabilitation within the target hubs will be supported by establishing nurseries. For physical dune fixation, interventions will utilise locally sourced, sustainably collected materials at minimal costs. By training target community members to install and maintain these interventions, the project will facilitate the scaling up of these measures across other hubs and areas in the country affected by sand inundation.

Establishing livestock cooperatives will support the expansion of sustainable livestock management. Capacity building for crop farmers, creating demonstration plots and providing training on climate-resilient seed varietal replication will help disseminate practices introduced by the project across the target hubs. Improved market access for goods produced through these livelihood interventions will further incentivise replication of these practices among adjacent communities, leveraging the benefits and traditional communication systems. Community WUGs will be capacitated by raising awareness about water supply and demand management practices in the target hubs. Such innovations — particularly water demand management — are cost-effective and easy to replicate, encouraging their uptake by neighbouring communities.

# D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Mauritania is classified as a least-developed country, with a gross domestic product (GDP) of ~US$7.9 billion and a GDP per capita of ~US$1,700.44 As a result of high poverty rates, particularly within rural areas, the population is heavily dependent on agriculture for subsistence; therefore, most households’ capacities to adopt alternative livelihoods are limited. The country’s Human Capital Index (HCI) score of 38, which ranks it 150th out of 157 countries, indicates that a child born in Mauritania can only expect to attain 38% of human capital by the age of 18, even if they

# D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed project is well aligned with Mauritania’s national adaptation priorities; it was designed in collaboration with project stakeholders at all levels.

Mauritania’s First Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), updated in 2021, identifies 19 objectives to be achieved by 2030. The project directly addresses five of these climate ambitions, namely:  
i) restoring natural grasslands;  
ii) conducting hydrological assessments and water resources availability analyses;  
iii) strengthening ecosystems’ resilience against climate change;  
iv) enhancing vulnerable populations’ resilience to climate change, particularly in  

---

[Page 33]

health and education; and  
v) improving food security and agricultural productivity.

The country imports 70–85% of its food requirements each year (accounting for 21% of GDP), despite agriculture being the third largest economic sector. The cumulative impact of these factors inhibits the capacity of national and local decision-makers to implement long-term strategies for responding to the adverse impacts of climate change on the country’s population.

The country’s high public and external debt — estimated at 79% of GDP and 68% of GDP, respectively — constrain the government’s ability to allocate funding from the national budget for CCA. Moreover, limited opportunities exist for public and private sector stakeholders to access financial resources for CCA, which is expected to cost ~USD$9.3 billion between 2015 and 2030. Given that this cost is likely to increase considerably under future climate change conditions, external investment is crucial to enhance the climate resilience of Mauritania’s population.

ii) facilitating sustainable natural resource management practices and the adoption of sustainable, diversified livelihoods by developing value chains; and  
iii) supporting the Go M in developing local, regional and national frameworks for mainstreaming climate change and gender-responsive adaptation solutions into plans and policies.

# D 
## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 35

The economic analysis estimated the net present value (NPV) of the net benefits (economic benefits less project costs) to be ~USD152 million (discounted at social discount rate of 2% over 20 years), with a benefit-to-cost ratio of ~5.8 (USD5.86 generated per USD1 spent), reflecting a cost-effective investment for the GCF and GoM.

Project benefits included in the economic analysis are:
- Food crops sustained for household consumption;
- Water security sustained for household consumption;
- Fruit and beverage resources sustained for household consumption;
- Livestock grazing forage sustained for household consumption;
- Agricultural income generation sustained for trading households;
- Natural products income generation sustained for trading households; and
- Income generation opportunities created through new goods and services for trading households.

### Health benefits

The NPV of health benefits sustained under the proposed GCF project is estimated at USD24.3 million. This estimate was determined by conducting a literature review to reveal the incremental healthcare cost for treating malnourished patients. The healthcare cost per capita in Mauritania was estimated using available data and the incremental cost was applied. The proposition behind this is that by implementing the GCF interventions, the livelihoods of the population in the target hubs improve, resulting in less malnourishment in hub communities. This leads to substantial cost savings, as literature suggests it is more expensive to treat patients who are/were malnourished.

Further details are available in Annex 3: Financial and Economic Appraisal.

### Rural livelihood benefits

A financial analysis of income potential sustained by the adoption of climate-smart agriculture, climate-resilient livestock management and alternative, natural resource-based livelihoods was carried out using best available data and research. This requested GCF grant investment will be supported by in-kind co-financing of US$2.6 million from the MEDD and regional authorities in the target hubs. Co-finance from the MEDD and regional authorities will be used to cover baseline costs, including operations and maintenance (which may be revised during project implementation) necessary to ensure sustainable land and water resources management. With the support of GCF grant funding, the proposed project will deliver several adaptation benefits using an integrated approach to EbA and sustainable natural resources management, contributing to a paradigm shift in the country’s approach to addressing the impacts of climate change-induced desertification.

Beyond the direct co-financing, the proposed project is strategically positioned to benefit from parallel financing totaling ~US$43.8 million, drawn from complementary initiatives within the target regions. These include:

- The Integrated Management of Protected Areas in the Arid Regions of Mauritania (IMPADRA) with around US$20 million
- The Sustainable Management of Natural Resources project (PROGRES) with an estimated US$6.6 million
- The Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme with approximately US$14.7 million
- A climate change adaptation project with around US$6 million in funding

The combined investment of ~US$63.8 million will be used efficiently and effectively to achieve two main project objectives:

1. Local, regional and national institutions will gain improved access to climate-risk information for strategic land-use planning and natural resource management that will, in turn, facilitate continued investment in ecosystem restoration, EbA, diversified livelihoods and sustainable natural resources management.
2. Communities in rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary will have sustainable access to food and water resources, reducing pressure from the impacts of climate change-induced desertification.

# E.1. Project/Programme Focus

# E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1 -2 pages)

## Assessment

### Dimension

#### Current state (baseline) Potential target scenario (Description)

There has been some progress in addressing climate change adaptation (CCA) and natural resource management in Mauritania through national and local climate change committees, plans and strategies.

National cross-sectoral coordination initiatives implemented towards improving climate change adaptation include the establishment of Regional Delegations for Environment and Sustainable Development (DREDDs) in each wilayah, the National Council for the Environment and Sustainable Development (CNEDD) and corresponding Regional Councils for Environment and Sustainable Development (CREDDs) in each wilayah, and focal points for climate change designated within most ministries within the GoM.

Locally, coordination has been improved through several donor-funded initiatives that have led to the strengthened capacity of Associations for Natural Resources (AGLCs) to implement EbA as well as the establishment of Climate Action Plans.

While coordination has improved through initiatives like DREDDs and AGLCs, clearer mandates are needed for both state and non-state actors to integrate CCA into sub-national and local legislation and policy.

### How the project/programme will contribute (Description)

The project aims to achieve the following outcomes across the four target wilayah, which include 43 communes outside rural-urban hubs and ~802,600 residents:

- Enhanced capacity and integration — clearer mandates for both state and non-state actors regarding integrating CCA into sub-national and local legislation, policy and improved adaptation funding. This target addresses the baseline's coordination gaps by providing specific responsibilities and frameworks for integrating CCA.
- Improved incorporation — incorporation and mainstreaming of climate change adaptation and natural resource management in community and decision-makers participation will be secured through the CTCs, WUGs, commune-level livestock cooperatives and other community level groups.

Once formally recognised by the Ministry of the Interior, CTCs can be established in all remaining communes, to strengthen coordination and knowledge-sharing — both within and across administrative levels — across Mauritania. Newly established CTCs will leverage the NAP Knowledge Management Platform to access and apply lessons learned and best practices from the proposed project. Through this platform, CTCs will be able to draw upon documentation of successful interventions, practical implementation guides and monitoring frameworks that have been tested in the initial target hubs. The platform will be particularly valuable for sharing region-specific approaches to climate change adaptation, allowing CTCs to streamline proven methodologies to their local interventions while avoiding implementation challenges identified by the proposed project, resulting in more efficient resource allocation. This will enable interventions to be upscaled in the target hubs, as well as implemented in the 43 non-target communes.

Community members will be trained to install and maintain dune fixation interventions, thereby facilitating the scaling up of these measures across other communes in the hubs.

## Sustainability

Nationally, public investment in CCA includes the Presidential Priority Program (PPP). This investment, however, is currently limited to less than 5% of the country’s annual GDP. At the wilayah-level regional funds for sustainable development also exist, but these vary considerably from year to year, making long-term planning for CCA investment difficult. Moreover, there are limited guidelines and policies dictating what percentage of this investment should be channelled towards CCA.

Private sector investment has somewhat alleviated gaps in public CCA investment. This investment includes PES schemes introduced to some areas by the World Bank and IFAD. These PES schemes, however, are limited in their physical extents.

# E.4. GCF Outcome level: Enabling environment (IRMF core indicators 5-8 as applicable)

## Core Indicator Baseline context (description)

Mauritania has made some progress towards addressing its resilience to climate change impacts through alignment with several multilateral environmental agreements — including the UNFCCC, CBD and CCD65 — as well as several national planning frameworks and strategies. Despite these efforts, there are still considerable gaps in the policy and institutional environments at the national, regional and local levels regarding climate change and natural resource management.

Climate change knowledge in general has not been fully institutionalised across Mauritania. Government officials at both the provincial and local levels often do not fully understand the impacts of climate change and how to effectively develop strategies to improve resilience. Additionally, staff from key sectoral organisations at the local level often have limited technical capacity to support CCA initiatives.

## Rating for current state (baseline)

- **Low**

## Target scenario (description)

How the project will contribute

- Create clearer mandates for both state and non-state actors regarding integrating CCA into sub-national and local legislation, policy and improved adaptation funding.
- Enhance decision-makers' use of knowledge on climate change impacts and adaptation responses, bridging the current gap between research and policy-making.
- Improved incorporation and mainstreaming of climate change adaptation and natural resource management into regional and local budgets, strategies and plans.
- Robust regional and local coordination mechanisms for implementing climate change adaptation.
- Improved access to financing to upscale EbA and other Climate change and sustainable management of natural resources will be mainstreamed into sub-national and local plans, policies and frameworks.

## Coverage

Multiple sub-national areas within a country

There are limited assessments on the impact of climate change and vulnerabilities of communities and ecosystems throughout most of Mauritania and specifically in the four target hubs. Information on losses and damages from climatic hazards, for example, are not quantified and readily available. Although some lessons learned have been generated from previous adaptation projects, there is generally limited knowledge on the implementation and benefits of CCA measures and natural resource management. Economic appraisals of potential cost-effective adaptation measures such as integrated land rehabilitation, water resource management, resilient crop species and livestock breeds have not been undertaken in Mauritania that would inform and promote the upscaling of these interventions.

###### Activity 1.1.1. Establish coordination platforms to facilitate knowledge-sharing, natural resource management, sustainable land-use planning and the implementation of EbA activities at the regional and local levels.

Activity 1.1.1 will enhance coordination and technical capacity for CCA across the four target hubs by forming commune-level technical committees (CTCs) in 12 priority communes.

Each CTC will be chaired by the respective commune's mayor, with members comprising of:
- DREDD representatives
- AGPO representatives
- Technical experts in hydrology, agriculture, livestock husbandry and other pertinent fields
- Civil society representatives
- Local community members

These CTCs will be capacitated through training workshops to enable them to design and implement site-specific CCA interventions.

To further support CTCs' activities:

###### 1.1.1.1: Establish commune-level technical committees (CTCs) within each priority commune in the four target hubs.

###### 1.1.1.2: Deliver training workshops to CTCs, enhancing members' capacities to implement and manage project activities, support the integration of climate change in regional- and commune-level policies, plans and budgets, and facilitate knowledge sharing between regional- and local-level stakeholders.

###### 1.1.1.3: Train CTC members and government staff to use the NAP Knowledge Management Platform and facilitate the collection and dissemination of climate information and adaptation best practices by CTC members.

# Activity 2.2.1. Establish community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) and commune-level water monitoring and regulation plans

## Under Activity 2.2.1, 12 WUGs will be established. WUG members will comprise local community members and representatives from livestock cooperatives, farmer-based organisations and women's groups in each target commune.

WUGs will be trained to monitor water availability and usage within their respective communes. Additionally, members will be trained to oversee and manage the construction, operation and maintenance of infrastructure for capturing surface water, managing runoff flow, increasing groundwater recharge and reducing flood risks.

A team of national experts will conduct hydrogeological studies to quantify and map groundwater and surface water resources within each of the target hubs. This will inform the selection of strategic sites to install:

- **i) groundwater dams**, for improving aquifer recharge;
- **ii) gabions, dikes and stone bunds** for reducing flood risks; and
- **iii) reservoirs, boreholes, well points and rainwater-harvesting systems** to increase rainwater capture and storage.

The selection of target sites for water management infrastructure will be summarised in the form of commune-level water management plans.

## Activity 2.2.1.1. Establish community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) within each target commune and train members to implement and maintain water-related activities introduced under Activity 2.2.2 of the project.

### Output 2.3. Climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods based on sustainable land- and natural resource-use are developed and/or strengthened to reduce land degradation and support climate-resilient income-generation by community members within the target regions.

#### Activity 2.3.1. Facilitate the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices and sustainable diversified livelihoods by farmers and community members within the target communes

Activity 2.3.1 aims to facilitate the adoption of sustainable climate-smart agricultural and livestock practices, as well as sustainable alternative livelihoods within the twelve target communes. Agricultural interventions will be focused on, inter alia, crop diversification, upscaling water-efficient irrigation systems and the adoption of small-scale fruit and vegetable production.

Climate-resilient livestock management practices to be supported by the GCF project will include:  
i) the development of rotational grazing plans;  
ii) the use of climate-resilient fodder species and livestock breeds; and  
iii) the establishment of water access points along historical transhumance routes.

The GCF project will further decrease communities’ dependence on rainfall-sensitive economic sectors by facilitating the adoption of alternative, sustainable livelihoods such as apiculture (beekeeping) and market gardening.

Climate-smart agricultural and horticultural measures will be supported by the establishment of nurseries and seed banks to provide climate-resilient crop varieties. Stock material consisting of plant cuttings and seeds will be supplied to the

#### 2.3.1.2. Collect cuttings and seeds from agricultural crop species, as well as indigenous grass and tree species, to serve as stock material for nurseries and seed banks established under Sub-activity 2.3.1.1.

#### 2.3.1.3: Train farmers within the target communes to practice climate-resilient crop agriculture and use improved agricultural technologies, including drip irrigation kits, solar powered pumps, integrated pest management strategies, zai pits and half moons.

#### 2.3.1.4. Conduct site visits and provide technical support to facilitate the uptake of sustainable livelihood activities — including horticulture (market-gardening), apiculture, poultry farming, livestock feed production and the collection and sale of non-timber forest products — by community members within the target communes.

## Activity 2.2.1: Technical support to community members through training on climate-smart agricultural practices, sustainable livestock practices and alternative livelihoods

Under Activity 2.2.1, technical support will be provided to community members through training on climate-smart agricultural practices, sustainable livestock practices and alternative livelihoods. ## Activity 2.3.2: Establish a small grants facility to facilitate continued investment in upscaling successful EbA activities and sustainable livelihoods

Under Activity 2.3.2, a project Small Grants Facility (SGF) will be established to enhance local communities’ access to finance to upscale successful EbA interventions and sustainable agriculture-based livelihoods introduced under the project. The on-granting mechanism will promote and support community-led initiatives, fostering the autonomous uptake of EbA practices by community members.

Regional government budgets in the four target wilayah will be inspected and based on this, budget briefs with recommendations for channelling a percentage of sustainable development funding towards the project SGF will be prepared, to promote government investment in CCA.

# E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A full time Monitoring Officer will be employed to conduct and coordinate monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities for the project. The Monitoring Officer will design and operationalise a performance monitoring framework to track the project’s progress towards achieving its targets. This will include:  
i) measuring performance against the project indicators (Sections E.3–5) to evaluate the progress of the project;  
ii) reporting the project’s performance to the PSC and PMU; and  
iii) providing technical support to the PC.  

Additionally, the Monitoring Officer will be responsible for overseeing and monitoring the application of gender-disaggregated indicators and ensuring that all gender targets are met. At particular milestones throughout the project — including annual performance reports, interim evaluations and at project conclusion — the project team will undertake evidence-gathering exercises to verify the progress. These internal reports will be further validated by reviews at the interim and end of the project implementation, as described below. UNEP will be responsible for managing the interim and the final evaluation of the project.

## Interim Evaluation

The UNEP Task Manager will oversee the process of hiring an external independent consultant to carry out the interim evaluation, who will provide an assessment of project performance against its targets at the project’s mid-point. This will be a formative exercise and will include analysing whether the project is on track, what problems and challenges the project is encountering, and what corrective actions are required so that the project will achieve its intended outcomes by project completion in the most efficient and sustainable way. The PSC will participate in the interim evaluation and develop a management response to the evaluation recommendations along with an implementation plan. It is the responsibility of the UNEP Task Manager to monitor whether the agreed recommendations are being implemented during the remainder of the project’s operational life.

## Final Evaluation

An independent consultant will be contracted by the UNEP Evaluation Office to conduct the final evaluation at the end of project implementation. UNEP’s Evaluation Office (EO) will be responsible for overseeing the final evaluation at the end of project implementation — which is a summative evaluation — and will liaise with the UNEP Task Manager throughout the process. An independent assessment of project performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, likelihood of impact and sustainability) will be made based on documentary evidence, stakeholder interviews and, in most cases, a field mission. The evaluation criteria will be aligned with the criteria contained in the GCF Evaluation Policy. Each evaluation criterion will be rated using a six-point rating scheme and a weighted average will be determined to provide an overall performance rating for the project as a whole. Where there are any differences in ratings between the evaluation team and the Evaluation Office, a final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office when the evaluation report is finalised.

# 6. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
|----------|-------------|--------|
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

High staff turnover within government institutions could impede capacity building, the retention of skills and knowledge, and knowledge-sharing across relevant institutions.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Communities and local authorities at different levels will be involved in the implementation of the proposed project. This will include the establishment of hub-level coordinating platforms, community-level decision-making groups, livestock cooperatives and community-level water-user groups through which information on — and ownership of — project interventions will be shared and maintained. As a result, knowledge regarding project planning and implementation processes will be spread across a range of individuals, thereby reducing the impact on the project if individuals leave their respective organisations.

Under the proposed project, institutional stability will be provided by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) established as part of the project management arrangements (see Section B.4 for more detail).

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
|----------|-------------|--------|
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

Climate-related hazards such as droughts, floods or sandstorms or other environmental hazards may disrupt or damage nature-based or physical structures aimed at increasing community resilience to climate change during their implementation. The survival of planted seedlings used for agricultural practices or ecosystem restoration is also likely to be threatened by climate threats such as drought as well as pests such as herbivorous mammals or insects.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Project interventions will be closely and continuously monitored to identify potential climatic and environmental hazards so that appropriate measures can be enacted should such an event occur. Agricultural planting and ecosystem restoration will be undertaken using drought-resilient species and will be undertaken during the cooler months. Water-efficient irrigation techniques such as bottomless cylinders and the use of mulch to reduce evaporation will also be considered to maintain seedling health. Where needed, supplementary planting of seedlings will be undertaken to replace lost plants. Enclosures and guards will be utilised to protect restored areas from herbivorous pests, with guards specifically aimed at prevent animal invasion and identifying any damages to the enclosures.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
|----------|-------------|--------|
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

Limited coordination between implementing partners reducing the efficiency of interventions.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

A project management unit and steering committee will be established to coordinate project implementation (Further details on the implementation arrangements are presented in Section C.7). At the hub level, coordination and management committees will ensure coordination among government institutions. At the community-level, user groups, decision-making groups and cooperatives will ensure coordination between communities and local authorities.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

### Category: Probability Impact
- **Governance**: Medium Medium

### Description
Limited participation by women and other marginalised groups in project interventions and decision-making could lead to the needs of these groups not being considered or represented in project design or implementation. In particular, similar previous projects have shown that limited female participation in formal training activities is a risk as a result of long travel distances and limited transport options for women.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
During project development, women and marginalized groups were consulted to ensure that their specific requirements are addressed. A GAP analysis was undertaken to ensure that interventions are gender-sensitive and that women and marginalized groups are represented in awareness-raising, training and other measures that benefit communities. Additionally, all members of the project management structures will receive gender sensitivity training to ensure that decisions that affect project implementation are socially and gender sensitive. Particularly for training activities, formal training workshops will be organised on-site to reduce the need for women to travel.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

### Category: Probability Impact
- **Governance**: Medium

### Description
Changes in political leadership at local level, as well as within ministries and other participating governmental institutions could result in delays in implementation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Even with more frequent changes in governing parties through elections and via personnel shifts in ministries, which are to be anticipated, the project will be fully anchored through the involvement of multiple ministries and institutions, ensuring project continuity without major interruptions. Specific mitigation measures which have been built into the project's design are mentioned below.

- Regular engagement with and capacity building of non-political actors — such as government agency officials, staff and local community members — will be prioritised during project implementation.
- Formulation of local ordinances (including community-level user groups, decision-making groups and cooperatives).
- Mainstreaming of project interventions into local planning, policy-making and budgeting systems will ensure that political challenges will have negligible impact on the sustainability of project interventions.

### Category: Probability Impact
- **Other**: Low Medium

### Description
Global shocks such as the Covid-19 pandemic or regional conflicts could influence government priorities, resulting in delays in project implementation and reducing the likeliness of project sustainability.

The cost relating to the large travel distances between sites has been accounted for in the project’s budget. Where possible, local implementing representatives will be used to coordinate the day-to-day implementation of the interventions to reduce the need for unnecessary travel between sites.

# 7. GCF Policies and Standards

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Project categorisation

The environmental and social risks associated with this project have been evaluated in accordance with the GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards. The proposed project is classified as Category B (moderate risk), based on the screening process and regulations used by the GCF.

Three of the 8 safeguard categories are deemed moderate risk:

### SS 1: Biodiversity, Ecosystems and Sustainable Natural Resource Management

The inclusion of Prosopis Juliflora as a potential species for biological dune-fixation is considered to be necessary risk within very specific contexts (such as highly mobile dune environments where indigenous species are not able to establish). However, there is a high risk of proliferation, and the potential difficulty of removing these plants is well established.

### SS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security

There are some health and safety risks inherent to undertaking construction activities, including for dune fixation and water infrastructure installation. These risks apply to project personnel, contracted labour as well community members residing in, contributing to, or traveling through, the vicinity of the construction activities. There are also some minor risks that rainwater harvesting and other water provisioning infrastructure could pose a health and safety risk to community members, related to the transmission of vector- or water-borne diseases that are associated with standing water or poor hygiene practices.

### SS 8: Labor and working conditions

Gender inequality is prevalent in Mauritania. While the project does include a Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAP), there may be certain instances where women will be inhibited from participating in specific employment opportunities due to cultural or social prohibitions.

Justification for this classification is provided in Annex 6: Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which contains an overview of the general project implementation risks that have been identified to date. A detailed risk rating for each activity is provided in Chapter 5 of Annex 6.

### Stakeholder consultation

To date, extensive stakeholder consultations have informed the project preparation process. The full Stakeholder Engagement Plan is presented in Annex 7: Stakeholder Engagement Plan, which includes the full list of stakeholders consulted and a summary of their feedback. The results of stakeholder consultations have closely informed the design of the proposed project and development of the ESMP. These consultations are expected to continue during the implementation phase, to ensure regular communication between project personnel and affected communities.

Accordingly, a participatory approach will be used when conducting E&S risk screening exercises, such that local community members' input is incorporated into activity-level risk registries and associated risk management strategies. As a minimum, it is recommended that specific Stakeholder Engagement Plans be developed for each project Outcome, since each activity set will have different consultation requirements, stakeholders and E&S risks.

### Project grievance redress mechanism

A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) has been developed for this project and are described in detail in Chapter 10 of the ESMP (Annex 6). The proposed mechanism is compatible with Mauritanian legislation and compliant with UNEP and GCF standards, enabling any project stakeholders with complaints or grievances related to the project to communicate their concerns through an appropriate process. The GRM will be available to stakeholders at all stages of the project and will provide an accessible, rapid, fair and effective response to concerned community members — including any vulnerable groups that may lack access to formal legal systems or support.

The GCF Independent Redress Mechanism will also be available to stakeholders and affected persons who have grievances or issues relevant to the project's activities.

All project grievances (both internal and external) will be resolved using the steps below, with the hub-level CTCs assuming most of the duties and responsibilities in this regard:

- Step 1. Receipt of complaint/grievance;
- Step 2. Acknowledgement of complaint/grievance;
- Step 3. Assessment, investigation and resolution of complaint/grievance;
- Step 4. Closure of investigation and redress process; and
- Step 5. Outcome of the corrective action is verified with the complainant.

# G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) for this project provides an overview of gender-disaggregated challenges, specifically those related to: i) women’s rights; ii) the gendered division of labour; iii) political participation; iv) access to land; v) access to justice; vi) gender-based violence (GBV); and vii) climate change impacts.

Among the proposed project’s target population, climate change disproportionately affects women and other socio-economically marginalised groups in Mauritania as a result of unequal access to productive resources. Although Mauritania has ratified the International Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the GoM has not yet lifted reservations applying to articles 13(a) and 16 of CEDAW, which relate to: i) eliminating discrimination in family benefits; and ii) requiring equality in marriage and family matters.

Gender inequality is apparent at the household level, with the country's Personal Status Code (PSC), established in 2001, serving as the primary codified law governing marriage and family relations in Mauritania. The PSC provides a marital framework based on male guardianship over women and children, where men are considered the lawful heads of their respective households. However, ~50% of households in the focal hubs of Aoujeft, Rachid and Tamcheket are headed by women, mainly resulting from divorce, widowhood, and male migration to urban areas for work. Statistics detailing the gender of household heads in Nema are not currently available. Additionally, the PSC states that men hold decision-making authority, while women are expected to assist in managing the family.

Mauritania’s national Labour Code reinforces these conventional gender roles by prohibiting women from working in occupations considered dangerous, arduous or immoral. Although the GoM has ratified International Labour Organisation conventions on discrimination and equal remuneration in the workplace, women's labour force participation remains low (31%), and they face a considerable gender pay gap.

Educational disparities exist, with lower primary school completion rates for girls (~56%) compared to boys (~64). Early marriage and childbearing jeopardise girls' chances of completing school, resulting in lower literacy rates for women (~57%) compared to men (~70%). Gender discrepancies in access to finance limit women's capacities to adapt to climate change. While the Constitution grants equal rights to men and women to open bank accounts, access credit and register businesses, cultural norms and gender roles limit women's participation. Women also face 'time poverty' as they spend considerable time on non-remunerated household activities, limiting their time available to diversify their livelihoods or participate in projects designed to improve their resilience.

In accordance with the GCF Updated Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan 2020–2023 (GCF/B.24/15), the proposed project will address gender dimensions within the four target hubs by incorporating the following measures into activities designed under the proposed project:

- The establishment of a gender-balanced project management unit (PMU);
- The recruitment of a Gender Officer within the PMU, to support and monitor implementation of the GAAP;
- Compulsory gender training for the PMU;
- The inclusion of gender-mainstreaming tasks into all relevant consultancy and partnership ToRs;
- The identification and strengthening of at least six women-led civil society organisations (CSOs), to assist with knowledge dissemination and recruiting women for activities and training workshops;

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65

## G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by UN financial regulations, rules and procedures, as well as UNEP’s Programme Manual. The financial rules of UNEP, which follow International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS), are promulgated pursuant to the Financial Regulations and Rules of the UN.

Within this context, funding-allocation mechanisms are managed as per the UN framework, including eligibility criteria, proposal evaluation processes, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is audited annually by the UN Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for GCF resources.

The funding of a project will be established through a distinct grant within the Trust Fund, with the project itself being set up in the UNEP Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) as a 'Work Breakdown Structure' (WBS), which is itself further broken down into 'Work Breakdown Structure Elements' (WBSEs), organised by output and outcome. The grant of the project will be linked to the WBSEs to fund the activities contributing to the delivery of specific outputs (as per the disbursement schedule detailed in the term sheet). The fee income will be managed through a dedicated grant independently and separately of the GCF project grant funds.

In line with UNEP procedures, the project will appoint a Financial and Administrative Officer within the PMU who will be responsible for monitoring, reporting on and approving requests for funds on a quarterly basis for the activities executed by the MEDD. Reports to summarise the disbursement and projected demands for project funding will be prepared and submitted to a UNEP Task Manager who will conduct project supervision, in line with reporting standards and methodologies applied in past projects, such as those implemented using GCF modalities.

The UN financial regulations and rules require the segregation of duties, and safeguards to ensure compliance with UN financial rules and regulations. In addition, a Fund Management Officer will be appointed to assist UNEP’s Project Officer with all financial monitoring and supervision functions. All procurement will be undertaken in line with UN procurement regulations, rules and policies.

In accordance with UNEP’s modalities for project implementation, funds will be transferred in tranches to the EE (MEDD), once the EE has satisfied all conditions defined under the legal/cooperation instrument — including the Project Cooperation Agreement (PCA), which will be signed between UNEP’s Climate Change Division and the MEDD. The PCA will include specific obligations for the EE on financial management and reporting and will require periodic financial reporting to follow international financial and auditing standards. The PCA specifically requires the audit be undertaken by a recognised firm of certified public accountants.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 76  

### Proposal name: Strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations in four regional hubs in northern Mauritania  

#### Accredited entity: United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP)  
#### Country: Mauritania  
#### Project size: Small  

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:  

#### Strengths  
- Ecosystem-based adaptation approach to restore ecosystems and stabilize dunes, utilizing green-grey infrastructure. This includes 2,123 hectares of dune fixation and 120 kilometres of protective fencing, benefiting approximately 230,237 people in total.  
- The four regions where the project activities will be implemented are not immediately adjacent but are within a few hundred kilometres of each other. If the distances between sites make implementation challenging, the project’s impact could be compromised.  
- The project promotes locally-led adaptation by actively involving communities in decision-making, ensuring context-specific, sustainable climate resilience measures that address local needs.  

#### Points of caution  
- The accredited entity does not have in-country presence in Mauritania, which could limit executing entity oversight.  
- The accredited entity has budgeted for a full-time national Chief Technical Advisor to mitigate this risk. In addition, the United Nations Environment Programme Task Manager will have biannual supervision missions.  
- The project will establish a Small Grants Facility to support the continued investment in upscaling successful ecosystem-based adaptation activities and climate-resilient livelihoods, providing financial support to local communities and organizations to help them adapt to climate change risks.  

### 2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed in the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE), and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/B.42/02.  

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment  

### 2.1 Project background

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 77  

### 3. Climate change is profoundly impacting Mauritanian communities, particularly those living along the Sahel-Saharan boundary, which extends from the north-west to the south-east of the country.  

A noticeable impact of climate change-induced desertification is the accelerated advance of sand dunes. Arable land, rangeland and pastures are being inundated, severely compromising the agricultural and livestock practices in the oases. In addition, sand inundation threatens critical socioeconomic infrastructure, including, inter alia, roads, school buildings and hospitals. In extreme cases, sand dunes have displaced entire villages, in some cases more than once.  

Additionally, the declining water table has dried up many pastoral wells, disrupting the traditional nomadic lifestyles of pastoralists and forcing them to adopt sedentary lifestyles around rural oases. This shift increases pressure on limited natural resources and heightens the risk of conflicts over resources.  

### 4. This proposed project focuses on addressing these climate-induced challenges by adopting an integrated ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach to restore ecosystems, prevent desertification, and enhance water and food security.  

Targeted interventions will be implemented across four regional hubs – Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema – located in the country's most climate-impacted regions. This approach includes establishing 2,123 hectares (ha) of green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure and 120 kilometres of protective fence line to facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem services related to dune stabilization and the supply of natural resources.  

Additionally, the project will develop or strengthen sustainable agriculture-based livelihoods and enhance water security.  

### 5. The project is expected to directly benefit 85,229 people (40,877 men and 44,352 women) and indirectly benefit 145,008 people (66,519 men and 78,489 women), reaching a total of 230,237 beneficiaries.  

The total project financing is USD 33,560,345, of which a GCF grant of USD 30,902,775 is requested. The Government of Mauritania will provide in-kind co-financing of USD 2,657,570.  

The project is submitted under the environmental and social safeguards Category B, and the total duration of the project is six years.  

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis  

### 2.2.1. Component 1: Governance and Capacity-building  

#### Outcome 1: Increased implementation and upscaling of ecosystem-based adaptation measures across the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania (total cost: USD 2.2 million; GCF cost: USD 2.1 million)  

6. Component 1 will be crucial in laying the foundation for long-term climate resilience and sustainable land-use practices in the Sahara-Sahel region of Mauritania.  

The component aims to integrate EbA approaches into governance and land-use planning, with a particular focus on strengthening the capacity of regional and local institutions to support climate-resilient interventions. It will work towards mainstreaming climate adaptation and EbA measures into government policies and budgets, particularly in vulnerable areas.  

7. A key feature of Component 1 is the establishment of 12 commune-level coordination and technical committees (CTCs). These CTCs will facilitate knowledge-sharing, natural resource management, and the implementation of EbA activities across the four target hubs.  

Each CTC will be composed of local stakeholders, including community representatives, technical experts and government officials. These committees will be trained to manage and upscale climate adaptation measures at the local level, ensuring that decision-making processes reflect the needs and priorities of local communities.  

8. Additionally, Component 1 will focus on the creation and dissemination of knowledge products such as training manuals, best practices and policy briefs. These products will aid in...

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 78  

The component also recognizes the importance of gender-responsive planning, aiming to ensure that women and vulnerable groups are actively involved in the planning and implementation of adaptation measures. The participatory approach will help to secure broad-based community ownership of the project interventions, which is essential for the project's success and continuity.

## Component 2: Strengthening Ecosystem Resilience and Climate-Adaptive Livelihoods  

### Outcome 2: Communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania have increased livelihood and water security as a result of investment in EbA measures, water access, and sustainable land and natural resources management practices (total cost: USD 28 million; GCF cost: USD 25.6 million)

10. Component 2 of the proposed project aims to significantly enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania. It focuses on EbA measures and other sustainable natural resource management practices to address desertification and improve livelihoods in vulnerable communities.

11. One of the core outputs under this component is establishing ~2,123 ha of green-grey dune-stabilization infrastructure and 120 kilometers of protective fencing around dune-fixation sites. These interventions will stabilize sand dunes and restore critical ecosystem services like soil stabilization and water infiltration, which will significantly reduce the impacts of desertification and enhance local biodiversity. These efforts will help mitigate sand encroachment on infrastructure and agriculture, leading to increased resilience and economic opportunities for local populations.

12. In addition to dune stabilization, the component focuses on improving water access for agricultural and land rehabilitation activities. This will be achieved by installing improved water management infrastructure, including rainwater harvesting systems and cisterns, enhancing water availability for both agricultural use and household consumption. This infrastructure will directly benefit communities by improving food and water security, which is crucial for their resilience against climate change.

13. The component will also support climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods by introducing sustainable land and natural resource-use practices. This includes training local farmers on climate-smart agriculture and alternative livelihoods such as beekeeping and poultry farming. The project will also assist in market access and develop value chains to help farmers diversify income sources, reducing their dependency on fragile ecosystems.

14. A Small Grants Facility (SGF) will be established to provide continued financial support for scaling up successful EbA interventions and sustainable livelihoods, fostering community-led initiatives and enhancing long-term resilience in the target hubs.

## Project management (total cost: USD 1.7 million; GCF cost: USD 1.5 million)

15. The project will be executed by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) in Mauritania, which will be supported by the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the accredited entity (AE). UNEP will oversee the entire project, ensuring that it aligns with GCF standards. As part of this arrangement, UNEP will also be responsible for managing the project's financial oversight, reporting and ensuring compliance with both GCF and UNEP policies.

### 16. A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will provide strategic direction, consisting of representatives from MEDD, key ministries, and local authorities. A project management unit (PMU) within MEDD, led by a Project Coordinator, will handle daily operations and reporting. The PMU will include specialists in financial management, procurement, gender, safeguard and monitoring. A Technical Committee, chaired by MEDD, will provide technical guidance.

### 17. In addition, implementing partners, such as local non-governmental organizations like Naforé, will be engaged for on-the-ground interventions in target hubs. Naforé will assist with ecosystem restoration, livelihood improvement, and water infrastructure activities. Other partners may be onboarded as the project progresses to enhance local implementation.

### 18. The GCF portion of this project management cost is less than 5 per cent of the total requested GCF funding and is compliant with the GCF Policy on Fees.

## Monitoring and evaluation component (total cost: USD 1.8 million; GCF cost: USD 1.8 million)

### 19. The project's monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements will be coordinated by a full-time Monitoring Officer, responsible for designing a performance monitoring framework. This framework will track progress against project indicators and ensure regular reporting to the PSC and the PMU. Gender-disaggregated indicators will also be monitored to ensure gender targets are met.

### 20. Key evaluation activities include an interim and a final evaluation. The interim evaluation, managed by UNEP, will assess the project's performance at its midpoint, addressing challenges and recommending corrective actions. The final evaluation, conducted by an independent consultant overseen by the UNEP Evaluation Office, will assess the overall project impact based on established evaluation criteria, including relevance, effectiveness and sustainability. These evaluations will be shared with stakeholders for feedback before public disclosure.

### 21. Furthermore, the project will produce annual progress reports and semi-annual financial reports. UNEP will manage these reports, ensuring they meet GCF standards. Field visits and supervision will be conducted by UNEP to ensure continuous monitoring and address any implementation challenges.

### 3.1 Impact potential    Scale: N/A  

### 22. The adaptation impact potential of the project is considerable, focusing on enhancing the resilience of communities and ecosystems along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania. The project is expected to directly benefit 85,229 individuals by providing improved protection against sand inundation, enhancing access to markets, income-generating opportunities, and clean water. Additionally, it will indirectly benefit 145,08 people through ecosystem restoration, better water management infrastructure, and strengthened climate adaptation capacity. The project aims to rehabilitate 2,123 ha of degraded land, enhance water security through improved infrastructure, and promote climate-resilient agriculture, with positive spillover effects across surrounding regions. This comprehensive approach will help increase resilience for the entire population in the four target hubs, benefiting approximately 230,237 people in total.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08

### 23. The project’s paradigm shift potential is considerable as it aims to create systemic changes in local climate adaptation practices through the establishment of coordination platforms and the integration of EbA into national and local policies. By creating these platforms and promoting knowledge-sharing, the project enhances the capacity of local and regional stakeholders to manage climate risks and implement climate-resilient land and water management practices. This can lead to the institutionalization of EbA approaches at the local level and create a foundation for long-term climate resilience.

### 24. However, the scalability of these solutions beyond the four target regions is largely dependent on broader institutional uptake and the availability of funding. While the project can serve as a demonstration model for future initiatives, its success in catalysing systemic change across Mauritania depends on the ability to secure continued financial support and the political will to integrate EbA into national policies. The wider replication of these practices will require strong engagement from national and regional institutions to embed the project’s outcomes into broader policy frameworks and funding mechanisms.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: N/A  

### 25. The project demonstrates strong sustainable development potential, providing multiple co-benefits that align with the GCF Investment Criteria and contribute to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will enhance biodiversity through ecosystem restoration activities and will also help to conserve biodiversity by mitigating desertification and preventing further ecosystem degradation. This aligns with SDG 15 (life on land), which focuses on protecting, restoring and promoting the sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems.

### 26. The project will also have a direct impact on food security by promoting climate-resilient agriculture and livelihood diversification. The project will strengthen local food production by training farmers in climate-smart agricultural practices and providing improved irrigation systems, thereby reducing vulnerability to climate shocks and enhancing SDG 2 (zero hunger). Additionally, the establishment of alternative livelihoods such as beekeeping and poultry farming will provide communities with diversified income sources, ensuring food availability even during challenging climatic conditions.

### 27. Furthermore, improved community health is a key outcome of the project. The restoration of ecosystems and improved water management infrastructure will enhance water quality and availability, reducing waterborne diseases and improving overall sanitation. Access to clean water, especially through communal water points and rainwater harvesting systems, will directly support SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation). The project also seeks to reduce health risks through better infrastructure protection from sand encroachment, which will safeguard vulnerable communities from respiratory diseases caused by dust storms and improve the general well-being of the population.

### 28. Additionally, the project emphasizes gender equality by ensuring that women are actively engaged in the planning, decision-making and implementation of climate adaptation interventions. This supports SDG 5 (gender equality), as women’s participation in leadership roles and sustainable livelihood initiatives helps to empower them economically and socially, while also contributing to broader community development.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: N/A  

### 29. The project effectively responds to the most pressing challenges for Mauritania by addressing the country’s high vulnerability to climate change impacts and the challenges related to desertification, water scarcity and economic development. As one of the most vulnerable countries globally (twelfth on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative Vulnerability Index)

30. The project tackles desertification directly by implementing EbA measures, such as dune fixation and ecosystem restoration, across 2,123 ha. This addresses one of the country’s most pressing environmental challenges, aiming to slow the spread of the desert into fertile agricultural areas and protecting livelihoods from sand encroachment.  

31. Water scarcity is another critical issue in Mauritania. The project improves water security by installing water management infrastructure, including groundwater recharge dams, solar-powered boreholes, and rainwater harvesting systems. Additionally, the project responds to the country’s low economic development by promoting climate-resilient livelihoods through sustainable agriculture, livelihood diversification (such as beekeeping and poultry farming), and strengthening food security.  

### 3.5 Country ownership Scale: N/A  

32. The project demonstrates strong country ownership by aligning closely with the national adaptation priorities of Mauritania and has been designed in collaboration with stakeholders at all levels. It directly addresses several key objectives from first nationally determined contribution of Mauritania, particularly in areas such as restoring natural grasslands, conducting hydrological assessments, strengthening ecosystem resilience, and enhancing the resilience of vulnerable populations, especially in rural regions. The project also contributes to the country’s national adaptation programme of action (NAPA), which encourages a cross-sectoral approach to climate adaptation. It directly addresses 19 of the 27 priority adaptation measures outlined in the NAPA, including EbA interventions, water management techniques, and capacity-building for communities affected by climate change.  

33. The project also supports the ongoing national adaptation plan development process in Mauritania, which began in 2018, by enhancing the capacity of both policymakers and vulnerable groups. This will include activities such as awareness-raising campaigns targeting local authorities, and training programmes for relevant government staff. Additionally, the project aligns with the country’s spatial planning strategy, which uses an oasis-centred administrative structure to enhance resilience to desertification and mitigate rural exodus.  

34. Extensive consultations with a wide range of stakeholders – including government representatives, civil society organizations, the private sector and local communities – have been conducted to ensure the project meets the country’s adaptation needs. As the national designated authority, MEDD has been actively involved since the inception of the project, contributing to the design process and validating the proposed interventions. Over 330 local community members from the target hubs participated in consultations, ensuring the project reflects the needs and priorities of the people of Mauritania.  

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: N/A  

35. The proposal presents a robust and context-specific economic and financial analysis (EFA), with clearly delineated methodologies for monetizing ecosystem services and estimating household-level returns. The EFA captures both market and non-market benefits across agricultural production, livestock management, water access and health system cost savings. The economic analysis utilizes an ecosystem service indexing approach, combined with macroeconomic contribution analysis (including multiplier effects and health-cost savings). For the financial analysis, a household-level analysis simulates with/without project scenarios, integrating productivity gains expected through GCF-financed interventions. The structure is consistent with GCF EFA guidance, and sensitivity analysis is included.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 82  

### 36. Long-term economic viability is supported by positive economic indicators.  
The economic analysis estimates a net present value of USD 151.5 million over a 20-year horizon, against project costs of USD 31.2 million. This yields a benefit-to-cost ratio of 5.86 and an economic internal rate of return (IRR) of 29 per cent. The analysis uses a 2 per cent social discount rate, lower than the GCF standard benchmark of 5–10 per cent, but justified given the non-market nature of ecosystem services and the strong resilience co-benefits delivered. The benefits encompass improved productivity in crop and livestock systems, enhanced access to water, economy-wide multipliers, and monetized health-cost savings from reduced malnutrition.

### 37. The financial analysis presents beneficiary-level cash flows and returns under “without” and “with” GCF support scenarios for oasis-based agriculture and livestock systems.  
With GCF interventions, the financial IRR improves from 12 per cent to 22 per cent (with a financial net present value of USD 42.6 million), indicating enhanced viability at household level. However, the analysis shows that most financial value accrues via subsistence consumption, limiting direct revenue generation. The EFA also incorporates a qualitative justification for 100 per cent grant support, citing multidimensional poverty, limited surplus income, low private sector participation, and fiscal constraints. The SGF is mentioned in the analysis, but it was not modelled in the financial analysis. Further development of activity-specific financial models for SGF-supported interventions would enhance the robustness and credibility of the analysis, particularly in demonstrating the financial viability and sustainability of these subprojects from a beneficiary perspective.

### 38. The adaptation cost per direct beneficiary is estimated at USD 362.6, and USD 134.2 per total beneficiary.  
These figures are above the GCF portfolio averages for Africa, which are usually under USD 200 per direct beneficiary. While the cost per beneficiary is relatively high, this is explained by the project’s focus on foundational EbA measures, and the inclusion of public goods such as water access and land restoration. The EFA demonstrates strong value for money by linking these cost levels to substantial economic benefits – with an estimated benefit-to-cost ratio of 5.86 and an economic IRR of 29 per cent. Overall, the economic and financial metrics reinforce the justification for full grant financing in the context of a least developed country.

The project has been classified as Category B, based the outcome of risk screening against the ESS standards that showed moderate to low risks. The environmental and social risk categorization is consistent with that of the revised ESP. ### 40. Biodiversity/Ecological:  
While the project interventions and associated outcomes have been designed to reduce degradation and improve the provisioning of ecosystem services,
