# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
Page 83  

There are ecological risks associated with the implementation of project activities. Poorly designed and implemented interventions are likely to baseline conditions and result in downstream impacts. This may lead to incidents of soil erosion, increased sand inundation, siltation of water resources, proliferation of alien invasives or affect normal hydrological functioning and the provision of ecosystem services for downstream communities. Further, the inclusion of *Prosopis Juliflora* as a potential specie for biological dune fixation, as it establishes faster than indigenous species, increases the potential of invasive species.  

The major threat to biodiversity and ecosystems remains dune stabilisation and the establishment of water infrastructure. It is expected that adopting mitigation measures proposed in the ESMP will reduce and eliminate potential risks.  

## 41. Labour and Working Conditions  
All activities requiring physical labour hold an implicit occupational health and safety risk, for project employees and implementing entities. Civil works such as construction activities, dune fixation and water resource desilting interventions pose labour and working condition risks. All physical activities or civil works including construction activities will be required to the proposed mitigation measures and acceptable best practices. This will require continuous training interventions, issuance and maintenance of suitable personal protective equipment (PPE), constant supervision and compliance monitoring by the employees themselves, their site management and the PMU.  

## 42. Community Conflict  
During project implementation incidents and issues related to financial mismanagement, lack of trust and lack of buy-in of project can also lead to potential inter- and intra-community conflict if perceptions of preferential or differential benefits stemming from project activities becomes an issue, or an observable reality. Stakeholder engagement is critical in to enhance transparency, accountability, regular feedback and information dissemination on project performance to beneficiary communities. This will reduce or eliminate potential risks associated with nepotism, fraudulent or corrupt practices as it can exacerbate community conflicts that have the potential to derail project success.  

Any potential social conflicts that may arise will need to be assessed and managed through the appropriate project consultation forums that will be constituted by relevant stakeholders (residents, commune council representatives and leadership etc.). Additionally, the GRM will serve as an important pathway for community members to voice concerns in an anonymous manner without fear of retaliation. Furthermore, existing tensions over water resources are well documented in the target hubs and the project will need to ensure that these realities and constraints are suitably identified and addressed during implementation.  

Effective stakeholder engagement and an operational GRM will place an emphasis on the early identification and resolution of avoidable conflict situations or scenarios.  

## 43. Involuntary Resettlement  
 Mauritanian law recognizes traditional property rights except when it interferes with the collective interest. During project implementation, it is expected that decisions, including the selection of sites for project interventions, will be undertaken through consultative and participatory processes to ensure they they do not lead the involuntary resettlement of any communities or community members. No involuntary resettlement is anticipated prior or during project implementation  

## 44. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and Environmental and Social Standard 7 (Indigenous Peoples)  
The AE has undertaken an assessment against the Indigenous Peoples Policy and engaged with authorities and civil society organizations to determine the presence of Indigenous Peoples within the project area, and confirms that none have been identified. The project is committed to inclusive and culturally appropriate engagement with all vulnerable and mobile groups through broader environmental and social safeguards and will establish a community-based coordination mechanism to facilitate management of resources.  

The project further indicates that, should Indigenous Peoples be identified during project implementation, an Indigenous Peoples Plan or an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework will be developed.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08

### 45. Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment  
The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM-2021/18 requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. The AE provided SEAH safeguarding in its submission to this funding proposal. The project ESMP identifies SEAH as one of the social risks and highlights key measures to address SEAH and gender-based violence (GBV) in alignment with the AE’s approach to SEAH and/or its equivalence.  

The risks in relation to SEAH have been identified from:  
(i) the outcomes of the UNEP risk screening;  
(ii) consideration of local legal requirements; and  
(iii) general good practice principles in the context of the proposed project interventions.  

Both annexes 6 and 8 have ensured that the approach to SEAH is survivor-centred. The AE noted that because of the absence of a legal framework for GBV in the country, several limitations affecting the GBV and SEAH grievance redress mechanisms have been identified. These include:  
(i) weak protection and accountability structures;  
(ii) limited standardized reporting mechanisms; and  
(iii) limited coordination with law enforcement.  

The AE has therefore put in place a SEAH management process (SEAH 9-step procedure) to ensure the safety of the survivors and continued psychosocial support services. In addition to this, the project will allocate SEAH focal points as well as the development of indicators to track the effectiveness of SEAH/GBV prevention and reporting systems.  

The PMU will comprise a gender officer to oversee any grievances related to GBV or SEAH. In addition, gender-related training will be provided to the PMU and include topics such as understanding and preventing SEAH and GBV.  

### 4.2 Gender policy  
46. The UNEP gender assessment for this project highlights the various challenges women and marginalized groups face, especially in the four regional hubs of northern Mauritania. The assessment describes the limiting factors for women including technical skills and access to means of production, such as land and credit, as well as gender norms and perceptions.  

The assessment describes potential risks for the participation of women in the project. Key gender project management outputs have been categorized as per each project outcome area.  

The project will ensure that there is a gender balance when establishing the PMU and management of its human resources. A gender officer will also be recruited in the PMU to support and monitor the effective implementation of the Gender Action Plan and oversee grievances related to SEAH/GBV.  

### 47. Activities to be implemented in this project include:  
- Gender training for the PMU;  
- Facilitating the participation of women in the project monitoring and learning;  
- Building women’s leadership and agency;  
- Ensuring that national policies, plans and strategies on natural resource management and land-use planning are gender-responsive;  
- Developing knowledge products on project lessons learned and best practices through a participatory process that engages the communities;  
- Demonstrating gender-responsive best practices in adaptation planning.  

The project will further document case studies featuring women's leadership in EbA interventions; provide gender-sensitive technical training on dune-fixation techniques; ensure at least 30 per cent female representation in water user groups; develop gender-sensitive water regulation plans that consider women’s water needs for domestic and agricultural use; ensure women’s participation in selecting locations for rainwater-harvesting systems and communal cisterns; and prioritize access to harvested rainwater for women-led smallholder farms and household use.  

It has been recommended that the AE should make any relevant mid-course corrections as and when applicable during implementation of the Gender Action Plan and leverage other opportunities where the gender targets, especially for women, could be increased given the social and cultural context.  

The Gender Action Plan consists of activities, performance indicators, baselines, targets, means of verification, budget, timelines and responsibilities.

### 4.3 Risks  

#### 4.3.1. Overall project assessment (medium risk)  
48. The Secretariat assesses overall programme risk as medium.  

#### 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)  
49. UNEP will serve as the accredited entity for this project. UNEP has experience in implementing environmental projects of this nature; it has 10 GCF-approved funded projects with a budget of USD 249 million in its portfolio. MEDD will act as the executing entity (EE). MEDD has been assessed for its capacity to manage the project effectively, with adequate financial management and procurement systems in place. However, the AE does not have an in-country presence in Mauritania, which could limit oversight of the EE. The AE has budgeted for a full-time national Chief Technical Advisor to mitigate this risk. In addition, the UNEP Task Manager will have biannual supervision missions.  

#### 4.3.3. Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)  
50. Implementation challenges: The four target regions where the project will be implemented – Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema – are not immediately adjacent but are within a few hundred kilometres of each other. The distance between these regions may present logistical challenges that could affect the efficiency and timeliness of implementation. To mitigate this, the project budget accounts for the logistical demands of coordinating activities across multiple sites, including transportation and communication.  

51. Lack of buy-in: There is a risk of resistance to the project activities due to a lack of understanding of the benefits or vested interests conflicting with the project goals. While consultations have been conducted to encourage community buy-in, ongoing engagement through awareness campaigns is necessary to address this challenge. A participatory approach will be integral to overcoming such challenges, ensuring that local communities take ownership of the project and are committed to its long-term success.  

52. Co-finance: Although co-financing from the government accounts for only 8 per cent of the total project financing and is in-kind in the form of staff time as well as office facilities to support the operations of the PMU, the government has committed USD 1.5 million for specific project activities, specifically to provide inputs required to cover 905 ha (42 per cent) of the total 2123 ha of dune stabilization to be undertaken. If the co-financing does not materialize fully, it could lead to delays and/or reduced scope of activities. The AE has secured co-financing commitment letters from the government, and it will be required to report to GCF on co-financing applied to the implementation of the funded activity.  

#### 4.3.4. Compliance risk (medium risk)  
53. The money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) risks associated with certain proposed project activities, such as green and grey infrastructure enhancements and the creation of an on-granting mechanism, are relatively elevated.   

54. The AE has assessed ML/TF risk as low based on its internal controls and governance mechanisms, as well as mitigation measures that will be implemented based on identified risks. For example, the AE’s due diligence of downstream implementing partners includes assessments of their governance and financial management capacities and a risk assessment that covers financial, operational, and reputational risks. The AE conducted a micro assessment of the EE/national designated authority under its Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers.

### 4.4 Fiduciary  
The project will be implemented by the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), as the accredited entity (AE), in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) of Mauritania, which serves as the executing entity (EE). UNEP will retain overall responsibility for project supervision, fiduciary compliance, financial oversight, and reporting to GCF. MEDD will be responsible for executing day-to-day project activities and managing country-level implementation in accordance with agreed plans. A dedicated Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established to coordinate project execution.  

### Oversight and strategic direction  
Oversight and strategic direction will be provided by a PSC composed of relevant government institutions, including the Ministries of Finance, Health, and Agriculture. A representative of UNEP will also form part of the PSC. This committee will have a decision-making capacity and will primarily serve to provide project oversight and advisory support, including:  
1. Overseeing project implementation; and  
2. Reviewing annual budget and workplans.  

### Financial management  
All financial management will be governed by the financial regulations and rules of UNEP. UNEP will manage GCF proceeds through a dedicated grant within its trust fund structure, and expenditures will be tracked through the UNEP enterprise resource planning system using a work breakdown structure. MEDD will submit quarterly financial requests for the activities under its responsibility, which will be reviewed and approved by UNEP. Financial reporting will follow international standards and UNEP policies, and will be supported by

# 4.6 Legal assessment

66. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 December 2016, and became effective on 20 February 2017, which was amended and restated pursuant to a first amendment and restatement agreement dated 13 July 2023, and which became effective on 2 August 2023 (the “AMA”)

67. The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

68. The proposed project will be implemented in the Islamic Republic of Mauritania, country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in the country may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

69. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

70. To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Overview  

This funding proposal 1 for a small-sized adaptation project in environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by the Government of Mauritania (GoM) through the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the proposed project’s accredited entity (AE). The executing entity (EE) is the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) of Mauritania. The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 33.56 million, of which USD 30.90 million is proposed as GCF finance and USD 2.66 million as in-kind co-finance from the GoM. The project implementation period is six years, while benefits are calculated over a lifespan of 20 years.

The proposed project’s objective is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems against climate change-induced desertification in the area of the Sahara–Sahel boundary within Mauritania. The project will focus on four rural–urban hubs (see the figure):  
- Aoujeft (in the Adrar wilayah, or region),  
- Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah),  
- Tamchekett (in the Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and  
- Nema (in the Hodh Chargui wilayah).  

The project is designed to help vulnerable communities and their settlements and livelihoods to adapt to climate change-intensified desert encroachment, sandstorms, heat, drought and flood, with a particular focus on combating sand inundation through the construction of “ecological infrastructure”.

# 5. Climate change risk and impacts

Observations of historical climate trends show that the mean annual temperature in Mauritania has increased by 0.3 °C per decade over the past 70 years – from 26.10 °C in 1950 to 28.46 °C in 2020². In addition to shifting temperatures, climate change in Mauritania has led to more frequent extreme rainfall events over short time periods (with associated flash floods), and a decline in the kind of low-intensity, long-duration rainfall that infiltrates into the ground), increased rainfall irregularity and more prolonged dry spells³. Precipitation has decreased during the wet season and over the entire year in Rachid, Tamchekett and Nema. In contrast, the Aoujeft hub has experienced a small but statistically significant increase in its overall precipitation. All four hubs have seen an increase in extreme daily rainfall, which often results in flash floods. The observed potential evapotranspiration (ETP) values are high in all four hubs (1,700–2,000 mm/year), with negative differences between precipitation and ETP, indicating that water loss via ETP exceeds water gained via precipitation. Sand movements are driven by the prevalence of strong winds from the north of Mauritania, including both the north-eastern trade winds and the harmattan – a dry wind strongest from late November to mid-March. Together, these winds carry large amounts of dust and sand across the country.

# 6. Future projections

In future, all four target hubs are projected to experience increases in both minimum and maximum temperatures under future Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5 (intermediate) and RCP 8.5 (high) greenhouse gas emissions scenarios, with frequency of heatwaves predicted to increase across all four hubs by 2050 under both scenarios⁴. The projected evolution of precipitation trends varies between the hubs. No significant change in precipitation is projected for Aoujeft by 2050 under either scenario, but annual precipitation is expected to significantly decrease in Rachid, Tamchekett and Nema, relative to the reference period (1981–2010), under both scenarios. In line with increasing temperature trends and decreasing precipitation trends, potential ETP is projected to increase across all four hubs by 2050 under both scenarios, and the water balance deficit in all four hubs is also projected to increase significantly (p < 0.1) by 2050⁵.

# 7. Drought projections

The combination of projected temperature rise, ETP and declining rainfall will increase the extent and severity of droughts at the national and local levels. By 2040, the median number of consecutive dry days for Mauritania is predicted to be between 219 and 230, compared to the historical value of 216 days in 1995. The Standardised Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI)⁶ displays a clear trend towards worsening drought conditions, with values below zero by 2040⁷. By 2100, the ninetieth-percentile values are expected to fall below −2, signifying an increase in extremely dry years. In its analysis, the AE has applied an assumption that wind conditions will remain constant under future climate scenarios in all four hubs. The combination of constant windy conditions and a generally warmer and drier climate is likely to facilitate the southward expansion of the Sahara into the Sahelian region of the country, further impacting the communities of the project’s focal hubs.

# 8. Vegetation loss and sand encroachment

Prolonged droughts across the country have also accelerated vegetation loss in desert environments, reducing ecosystem services related to soil stabilization and water infiltration. As a result, the rate of dune advancement along the Sahara–Sahel boundary has increased and greater sand encroachment has been observed in oases, with subsequent impacts on water resources, agricultural productivity and income generation across northern Mauritania. Arable land, rangeland and pastures are being inundated, as is infrastructure such as roads, schools

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 92  

and hospitals, with sand sometimes covering entire villages. Siltation of dams, boreholes, wells and rivers has reduced the accessibility and availability of already scarce water resources in the region. This is combined with reduced rainfall and water infiltration resulting from vegetation loss increasing the depth of the water table. This has led to many pastoral wells drying up, impacting pastoralists’ abilities to maintain nomadic lifestyles. Consequently, many nomadic pastoralists are adopting sedentary lifestyles around rural oases, increasing the pressure on limited natural resources, and heightening the risk of conflicts over resources.

### 9. Adaptation impact  
The adaptation impact potential of the project is considerable, focusing on enhancing the resilience of communities living in and around oases in the vulnerable interface between the arid Sahel zone and the hyperarid Sahara Desert to drought, flood and desert encroachment. Through Output 2.1, the exposure of urban and rural communities in the target hubs to sand inundation, escalated by climate change, will be reduced. This will be achieved through enhanced ecosystem services generated by physical dune fixation using a wattling technique to create a stabilized grid, fenced in to allow full establishment, and then mulching and vegetating these dunes with grasses, shrubs and trees to create permanent buffers against sand. These measures will reduce the vulnerability of water sources, agricultural production and human respiratory health to sand encroachment and dust storms. A set of grey-green adaptation measures will be used to reduce vulnerability to flood and drought, installing (a) groundwater dams, to improve aquifer recharge; (b) gabions, dykes and stone bunds, to reduce flood risk; and (c) reservoirs, boreholes, well points and rainwater-harvesting systems, to increase rainwater capture and storage. This will be complemented through Output 2.2, which will provide (a) training and inputs for climate-resilient crop agriculture and natural resource-based livelihoods; (b) training on climate-resilient and sustainable livestock management practices; (c) communal boreholes equipped with solar pumps in graras and along historical transhumance routes, as well as rainwater-harvesting tanks; and (d) support to community-managed water user groups in the form of commune-level (see paragraph 10 below) water management plans.

### 10.  
The project is expected to directly benefit 85,229 people (40,877 men and 44,352 women) living within 12 priority communes across the four target hubs, who will directly experience improved protection against sand inundation, enhanced access to markets and income-generating opportunities, and increased access to clean water. Additionally, the AE estimates that there will be about 145,08 indirect beneficiaries (66,519 men and 78,489 women), representing individuals in surrounding or non-priority communes who will experience knowledge-sharing benefits, improved coordination at the regional level, and access to the small grants facility for scaling climate change adaptation (CCA) interventions (around 3 per cent of the country’s total population and 12 per cent of the target wilayahs). The project aims to rehabilitate 2,123 hectares of degraded land, enhance water security through improved infrastructure and promote climate-resilient agriculture. This comprehensive approach will help to increase the resilience of the entire population in the four target hubs, benefiting approximately 230,237 people in total.

### 11. Risks to new ecological infrastructure  
In an exchange of written questions and responses, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) asked the AE to comment on two questions. The first related to the short-term prospects of the success of revegetation efforts in the face of the high winds, extreme temperatures and erratic rainfall likely to be experienced while the new ecological infrastructure is being established, as well as the potential scarcity of water for irrigation in the early stages. The second related to the long-term prospects of success of the artificial dune structures and green belts in preventing inundation of settlements.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 93  

### 12. In response to the question on the shorter-term risks to successful impact, the AE stated  
that “planting of greenbelts will be informed by best practices and lessons learnt from previous  
greenbelt initiatives in Mauritania, including several GEF [Global Environment Facility] projects  
and the FAO working paper (Fighting sand encroachment: lessons from Mauritania10). This will  
include using native species adapted to low soil moisture and with deep taproot systems…  
implementing rainwater harvesting infrastructure to supply additional water to greenbelts.  
Greenbelt trees will be…grown to more survivable sizes in nurseries and planted immediately  
following rains during the rainy season…Additionally, tree mortality during establishment is  
anticipated, and dead trees will be restocked.” UNEP highlighted that proximity to a water  
source was one criterion for dune stabilization site selection and that the project will establish  
boreholes for this purpose where necessary, as well as promoting measures for aquifer  
recharge. “The initial watering phase will employ gravel-filled bottomless cylinders to direct  
water to the roots and minimise evaporation, and the surface layer immediately surrounding  
the plant will be covered in mulch or other water-retaining products to further increase  
moisture retention.”  

### 13. On the longer-term question, the AE stated: “Assuming that trees and perennial  
vegetation are successfully established by the end of the 6-year implementation period, the  
prognosis for the ecological infrastructure over the 20-year project lifespan is generally  
positive. If favourable ecological, institutional and social conditions are maintained, restored  
dunes can remain stable and functionally intact for decades. Furthermore, the areas will be  
protected by law under the Forest Code, as areas revegetated by the State.” In addition to  
outlining lessons learned from similar initiatives in the Sahel12 about the need for water  
availability and effective protection of new infrastructure from grazing, the AE highlighted the  
need for careful species selection – for example, non-native Prosopis juliflora is one of the  
species with the best chance of surviving harsh desert conditions, but if poorly managed can  
become invasive.  

### 14. Use of SWAT model. The iTAP notes that the AE used a soil and water assessment tool  
(SWAT+) for hydrological modelling to evaluate the potential impacts of water resource  
management strategies by comparing a business-as-usual scenario with a scenario with  
interventions (involving installation of physical structures to manage and increase water  
infiltration). The analysis focused on identifying spatial patterns of run-off generation,  
infiltration potential and water balance components, to generate for each potential site the  
expected difference between scenarios, indicating where investment would be most effective.  

### 15. Chapter 7 of the feasibility study (annex 2) outlines the SWAT+ model set-up (inputs,  
baseline and intervention runs) but fails to include calibration of the model and its validation  
against measured streamflow or observed flood events. In response to a question about this, the  
AE acknowledged this gap, which was a result of limited availability of continuous and reliable  
discharge records at appropriate spatial resolution across the study domain. As a result, the AE  
relied on spatial differentiation in run-off and evaporation regimes as proxies for identifying  
hydrologically responsive sub-basins suitable for engineered or ecosystem-based interventions.  
Because it was not possible to calibrate and validate the SWAT+ model against observed flows,  
its outputs were not used for site selection; instead, the AE conducted a multi-criteria risk  
assessment, which was then validated with stakeholders.

### 17. The proposed project aspires to have “four paradigm-shifting pathways, as identified in the GCF sectoral guidance, aimed at enhancing the climate resilience of ecosystems, and the agricultural and water sectors.” These pathways include:  
1. transformational planning and programming;  
2. catalysing climate innovation;  
3. mobilising finance at scale; and  
4. establishing coalitions and enhancing knowledge to scale up successful outcomes.  

However, these approaches will be applied at sub-national level, in the communes of the four target hubs and the four wilayahs within which they fall.  

### Activity 1.1.1 (“Establish coordination platforms to facilitate knowledge-sharing”) is limited to wilayah-, moughataa- and commune-level development plans, policies and budgets in the target hubs.  

### Subactivity 1.2.2.3 does cover the dissemination of knowledge products in non-target communes across the four project wilayahs, but does not go beyond this to the country’s other eight wilayahs or the national policy level.  

### 18. Knowledge-sharing and learning. Numerous project studies and assessments will be conducted within the target hubs to improve the country’s climate change knowledge base, including assessments of value chains, surface and groundwater quality and quantity, and community vulnerability (disaggregated by gender).  

All the assessments will contribute to a CCA knowledge management platform, which will be used as a centralized repository accessible to stakeholders across different administrative levels.  

Best practices on CCA will be collated, tailored and incorporated into knowledge products, informing the design and implementation of subsequent adaptation interventions.  

This approach to knowledge management is intended to support both vertical scaling (enhancing the impact within the target hubs) and horizontal scaling (supporting CCA in other hubs, countries or regions) of successful adaptation measures.  

These knowledge products will also be integrated into the country’s national adaptation plan (NAP) knowledge management platform, which manages knowledge related to desertification and climate change in Mauritania.  

### 19. Enabling environment. At commune level, processes will be put in place to achieve a paradigm shift in the way resources are managed – including establishing commune-level technical committees and water user groups, strengthening local institutional and regulatory frameworks for monitoring water use and availability, training farmer organizations (including women) on climate-smart agriculture, and establishing a small grants facility to recycle some of the proceeds from sustainable livelihood activities back into adaptation beyond the project implementation period.  

This new approach is not reflected at the national level, however.  

In the project’s theory of change, it is suggested that climate change resilience will be enhanced “if supported by transformational change in policies, planning, knowledge management and capacity-building”, but the project focus only at the local and regional levels limits such change.  

### 20. The AE acknowledges that the project “takes a bottom-up approach to achieving transformational change and the integration of EbA [ecosystem-based adaptation] approaches into national and sub-national policies” and “lays the foundation for the integration of EbA approaches into wilayah-, moughataa- and commune-level development plans.”  

This is to be achieved through the development and dissemination of knowledge products, including policy briefs, but there are no planned points of formal engagement with national government and other regional government agencies that would allow for long-term changes to policy and financing frameworks for Mauritania as a whole, particularly for other oases and other areas vulnerable to sand encroachment.  

This seems a missed opportunity for the GCF project to contribute to transformational change by fostering long-term policy reforms in natural resource management.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential                                           Scale: N/A  

22. The proposed project is aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, contributing to a wide range of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), namely: SDG 2 – Zero hunger; SDG 3 – Good health and well-being; SDG 5 – Gender equality; SDG 6 – Clean water and sanitation; SDG 8 – Decent work and economic growth; SDG 10 – Reduced inequalities; SDG 11 – Sustainable cities and communities; SDG 12 – Responsible consumption and production; SDG 13 – Climate action; SDG 15 – Life on land; and SDG 16 – Peace, justice and strong institutions.  

Specific co-benefits anticipated from project interventions are discussed below.  

23. Environmental co-benefits. Capturing rainwater and allowing infiltration through on-the-ground water-harvesting structures, and better rangeland management, may allow for natural regeneration of seedbanks and root systems in degraded grazing lands, enabling native species to flourish again. With mostly native species to be used for revegetation, this will also have benefits for biodiversity. Dune stabilization and revegetation will reduce erosion of productive soils and land degradation, and will enhance soil organic carbon, as above- and below-ground biomass increase.  

24. Socioeconomic benefits. Support of climate-resilient agriculture, forest products and agriprocessing is expected to improve households’ nutritional status, reducing dependence on imports and, in some cases, food aid. Dune stabilization efforts should reduce the prevalence of respiratory diseases (such as lung cancer) and respiratory infection resulting from exposure to airborne dust, decreasing healthcare costs for households and government. Improved access to clean drinking water in the target hubs should lower the number of epidemics linked to waterborne diseases. Overall, there should be reduced losses from damage to property, infrastructure and crops, as well as increased lifespan and capacity of water infrastructure (such as weirs, gabions and wells). Improving access to water may have positive social effects, including reduced conflict over water resources and reduced risk of gender-based violence. It may also free up women’s time, as women will no longer have to travel far from their households to collect water, and improve school attendance for girls, who frequently leave school to fulfil the task of collecting water.  

25. Gender and social inclusion. The gender assessment and action plan (annex 8 to the funding proposal) highlights that although women in the rural areas of Mauritania are involved in subsistence farming, food processing and livestock management, they have limited access to land, financial resources, training and agricultural inputs. Less than 10 per cent of women have access to formal agricultural extension services, even though up to 50 per cent of households are headed by women in many areas. This limits women’s capacity to improve productivity and resilience to climate impacts. Section 1.13 of annex 8 also highlights the ongoing challenges of social inclusion in a country with a complex past characterized by enslavement of parts of the population. The assessment notes that “women are responsible for a considerable amount of household work, including agricultural production, post-harvest activities, collecting fuelwood and water, food preparation and childcare” and acknowledges the need to avoid additional burdens on women through new climate-smart agricultural practices, sustainable livelihoods and water management techniques.  

26. The proposed project interventions will address gender equality through several pathways. One will be to incorporate a gender action plan into new development plans and community climate action plans, based on the specific gaps identified within each community.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 96  

Another will be to integrate women into the project-specific institutional structures and involve them in decision-making. Women will be included in all CCA training, ensuring equal opportunity and access for on-the-ground adaptation interventions. Women will form at least 50% of the beneficiaries of technical assistance on new climate-resilient livelihoods, while awareness is maintained of the need to balance such new tasks by freeing up other time – for example, by reducing how far women need to walk to collect water (see paragraph 24 above).  

## 27. Risk of overextraction of groundwater  
The project’s lengthy and detailed feasibility study (annex 2) notes that groundwater extraction in oases is hindered by low levels of aquifer recharge, with a continually falling water table; that enforcement of regulations to control water extraction is limited; that overuse adversely affects marginalized groups, particularly women; and that baseline assessments conducted during project development highlight the risk of groundwater depletion when the use of solar-powered pumps is not strictly monitored. The funding proposal includes hydrogeological studies and maps in subactivity 2.2.1.3 as the basis for the appropriate siting of four new boreholes to be drilled per community, and the iTAP welcomes these measures. But there is no discussion of ongoing water-level monitoring and community governance of pumping in order to avoid unsustainable extraction, which is nowhere treated as a project risk. The iTAP believes this is an oversight. It recommends that this issue be included in the environmental and social management plan and that a subactivity be added to establish a governance system to determine and enforce pumping times and volumes, to monitor changes in the water table, and to take action to further restrict pumping if water levels fall beneath a predetermined threshold.  

## 28. The iTAP assesses the sustainable development potential of the project as medium to high.  
### 1.5 Needs of the recipient  
Scale: N/A  

## 29. Mauritania is classified as a least developed country, with a gross domestic product (GDP) of about USD 7.9 billion and a GDP per capita of about USD 1,700.  
As a result of high poverty rates, particularly within rural areas, the population is heavily dependent on agriculture for subsistence, with most households’ capacities to adopt new climate-resilient livelihoods being limited. The country’s Human Capital Index score of 38, which ranks it 150th out of 157 countries, indicates that a child born in Mauritania can only expect to attain 38 per cent of maximum potential human capital by the age of 18, even if they receive complete health and education, with the country still in the “low human development” stage despite significant progress over the past 30 years. In 2019, Mauritania was ranked the country twentieth most vulnerable to climate change and other global challenges, according to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative country index. With the highest global sensitivity score (0.602), Mauritania faces substantial insecurity in both the food sector and the water sector.  

## 30. Between 1900 and 2018, natural hazards such as droughts, disease epidemics, insect infestations and storms (strong winds and heavy rainfall) impacted more than 11.5 million people in Mauritania, according to the Emergency Events Database. In 2019, an estimated 33,600 Mauritanians were impacted by floods, while in 2020 approximately 609,000 people were affected by droughts. Flooding alone caused an estimated USD 5.5–11.4 billion in damages and losses between 2000 and 2008. Under future climate change scenarios, Mauritania is expected to face an overall decrease in water availability of 10–15 per cent; an increase in land degradation and erosion; continued loss of arable land through southward expansion of the Sahara Desert; a decrease in rangeland availability for livestock; and a reduction in food availability.

## Page 97  

31. The country’s high public and external debt – estimated at 79 per cent of GDP and 68 per cent of GDP, respectively – constrain the ability of the GoM to allocate funding from the national budget for CCA. Moreover, limited opportunities exist for public and private sector stakeholders to access financial resources for adaptation, which is expected to cost around USD 9.3 billion between 2015 and 2030. Given that this cost is likely to increase considerably under future climate change conditions, external investment is crucial to enhance the resilience of the population of Mauritania to the impacts of climate change – which are projected to intensify in the future – on food and water security, economic growth, and human health. The project will effectively respond to the most pressing challenges Mauritania is facing, addressing the country’s high vulnerability to climate change impacts and its challenges related to desertification, water scarcity and economic development.  

32. The vulnerability of the population of Mauritania is high because of elevated levels of poverty and a strong dependence on climate change-sensitive sectors, including agriculture, fishery, mining and livestock. In addition, access to basic services and facilities is unequal between rural and urban areas, and those attempting to transition out of poverty encounter major obstacles. As a result, approximately 6 per cent of the population of Mauritania live below the international poverty line (USD 1.9 per day in 2011 with purchasing power parity) and about 28 per cent live below the national poverty line. Moreover, 70 per cent of the country’s impoverished households live in rural areas and are mainly engaged in agriculture and livestock-rearing. This high prevalence of poverty and limited access to basic resources means that rural communities living within the four target hubs lack the adaptive and financial capacity to invest in climate-resilient sustainable development and are almost entirely dependent on external support from both government and development partners.  

33. Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are rated as high.  
### 1.6 Country ownership  
Scale: N/A  

34. The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultation with the GoM and a range of stakeholders, and is aligned with the policies of Mauritania. The roles of the AE and EE, as well as those of technical partners who will support implementation, are clearly set out in the funding proposal.  

35. Policy and strategy alignment. The proposed project aligns with the Mauritania national spatial planning strategy, which aims to strengthen resilience using an oasis-centred administrative structure to prevent rural exodus and enhance resilience to the impacts of climate change-induced desertification. The project is well aligned with the national adaptation priorities of Mauritania, as expressed in the country’s nationally determined contribution (NDC) to the Paris Agreement, updated in 2021. The project directly addresses five of the objectives of the NDC, namely:  
(a) restoring natural grasslands;  
(b) conducting hydrological assessments and water resource availability analyses;  
(c) strengthening ecosystems’ resilience against climate change;  
(d) enhancing vulnerable populations’ resilience to climate change, particularly in rural areas; and  
(e) strengthening institutional and technical capacities at local, regional and national levels to enable more effective planning, financing and implementation of adaptation measures.  

36. The project’s objectives are also consistent with the cross-sectoral approach set out in the country’s 2004 National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), which includes the following priority adaptation measures:  
(a) enhancement of knowledge of surface water’s hydrological cycle;  
(b) construction of flooding breakdown dykes in pluvial and oasis zones;  
(c) promotion of water-saving techniques in oasis zones;  
(d) participatory reforestation for agroforestry in agricultural zones; and  
(e) reorganization and capacity-building in communities adversely affected by climate change.  

The project is complementary to the country’s ongoing GCF-supported NAP development process, initiated in 2018. As such, it will build on efforts to enhance the capacity of policymakers, GoM staff and vulnerable groups, and will conduct

As the AE, UNEP has facilitated dialogue between national consultants, the MEDD, other relevant institutions and community stakeholders, ensuring that the project responds to the adaptation priorities of Mauritania. UNEP organized community consultations through four field missions (each covering all or most of the Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamchekett and Nema hubs) over a two-year period, consulting 330 local community members across the target hubs.

UNEP has a broad global portfolio of climate change projects funded via GCF, the Adaptation Fund, the Least Developed Countries Fund, the Special Climate Change Fund, and bilateral funds such as the European Commission. UNEP also has a long-standing relationship with Mauritania, having supported the country in the development of the NAPA, the GCF NAP Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme and several adaptation projects including two GEF-funded projects.

Since no mention is made in the funding proposal of the MEDD having executed a project of this nature previously, or of a specific capacity assessment in this regard, it is welcomed that the exit strategy (section B.6) mentions that “the project will work closely with the designated government entity to build its capacity in fund management, monitoring and evaluation, as well as in the strategic allocation of resources towards high-impact EbA interventions. This collaboration will include the development of clear guidelines for fund disbursement, criteria for project selection, and mechanisms for tracking and reporting on the impact of funded projects.”

Since its inception in 2009, Naforé has implemented several projects in the domains of environmental protection, health, hygiene and sanitation, showcasing its capacity through diverse projects and a wide range of expertise among its members.  

41. Country ownership of the project is thus seen to be medium to high.

# GCF/B.42/02/Add.08  
## Page 99  

42. The GoM is requesting USD 30,902,000 in grant finance from GCF, to be supported by in-kind co-financing of USD 2,657,000 from the MEDD and regional authorities. This co-financing will be used to cover baseline costs, including the operations and maintenance necessary to ensure ongoing sustainable land and water resource management. The GCF funds form 92 percent of the total project costs, while the co-finance forms 8 percent. This co-finance figure is low but reflects severe budgetary constraints faced by the GoM. The request for 100 percent concessionality is justified in section B.5 of the funding proposal, in relation to the high level of government indebtedness and the public goods nature of the ecological infrastructure investment at the heart of the project.

43. Beyond the direct co-financing, the proposed project is strategically positioned to benefit from parallel financing totalling around USD 41.3 million, drawn from complementary initiatives within the target regions. These include the Integrated Management of Protected Areas in the Arid Regions of Mauritania (IMPADRA) project (USD 20 million), the Sustainable Management of Natural Resources (PROGRES) project (USD 6.6 million), and the Africa Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme (AICRMP) (USD 14.7 million).19 The combined investment will be used to support local, regional and national institutions to improve access to climate-risk information for strategic land-use planning and natural resource management, and continued investment in ecosystem restoration, climate-resilient livelihoods and sustainable natural resource management, reducing pressure from the impacts of climate change-induced desertification.

44. The feasibility study (annex 2) provides a thorough analysis of recent and current related initiatives, lessons learned and proposed synergies. Although the project will support commune-level coordination platforms, it is not clear how national-level coordination with the many related initiatives will be undertaken to maximize effectiveness. As noted in the feasibility study, the GoM is actively engaged in the Great Green Wall (GGW) effort through its National Agency for the Great Green Wall, and there are significant opportunities for synergies and exchange between this proposed project and ongoing and planned GGW-related initiatives in Mauritania. A coordination mechanism for all these related projects, through the MEDD coordination cell, is recommended.

45. Return on investment. Both an economic analysis and a financial analysis (see annex 3 to the funding proposal) were conducted to inform the development of the project. The economic analysis determined the costs and benefits associated with implementing EbA interventions, including dune fixation and land rehabilitation activities; facilitating sustainable natural resource management practices and the adoption of new climate-resilient livelihoods by developing value chains; and supporting the GoM in developing local and regional frameworks for mainstreaming climate change and gender-responsive adaptation solutions into plans and policies. The economic analysis estimated the net present value (NPV) of the net benefits (economic benefits less project costs) to be USD 152 million (discounted at a social discount rate of 2 percent over 20 years), with an economic internal rate of return (IRR) of 29 percent and a benefit-to-cost ratio of 5.86 (USD 5.86 of benefits generated per USD 1 spent), reflecting a cost-effective investment for GCF and the GoM. A financial analysis of income potential through the adoption of climate-smart agriculture, climate-resilient livestock management and new climate-resilient livelihoods was carried out using the best available data and research. This generated an NPV of USD 31.16 million with a financial IRR on these activities of 22 percent.

46. ### 47. Financial sustainability  
With regard to financial sustainability post-project, the AE’s exit strategy (section B.6 of the funding proposal) indicates that several financing opportunities at the government and community levels will enable the continued operation and maintenance of interventions beyond the project’s lifespan: “At the government level, the prioritisation of CCA, gender considerations and natural resource management into policies, plans and strategies will promote the allocation of sufficient funds to CCA initiatives through national, regional and local government budgets.” This seems optimistic, since the AE has simultaneously noted in section B.5 that “the high level of public debt (99% of GDP in 2016) constrains public financing for CCA, making it difficult for the GoM to secure financing for adaptation projects” and that “further investments to address climate change are almost entirely reliant on assistance from external multilateral development partners, including the GCF”.

### 48. Sustainability of small grants  
According to the funding proposal, a critical component of achieving financial sustainability will be the project’s extension of an existing MEDD-based small grants facility (SGF) under Activity 2.3.2, designed to provide financial stability and facilitate continued investment in upscaling successful EbA activities and sustainable livelihoods. In response to a question from iTAP about how realistic it would be for the GoM to replenish this fund, the AE stated that “a modest level of replenishment of the SGF funding from government would be realistic to expect. However, additional funding sources will also be sought to support the continuation of the SGF after the project ends, including through linkages with other financial mechanisms and instruments. This will be in line with the long-term financing strategy for the SGF, to be developed under project sub-activity 2.3.2.2.”  
This area will need more attention during project implementation, in order to ensure that the fund is replenished, ideally with a long-term sustainable revenue source.

### 49. The project’s overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated as medium.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment (FP266)

## Proposal name: Strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations in four regional hubs in northern Mauritania

## Accredited entity: United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP)

## Country: Mauritania

### Impact potential
We acknowledge iTAP’s assessment of the project’s impact potential as “high”. We note that no specific recommendations are made in this regard.

### Paradigm shift potential
We acknowledge iTAP’s conclusion that the project’s paradigm shift potential is “medium” owing to the project’s focus being at the sub-national level, potentially reducing its ability to effect transformational change in national policies and planning.

As noted in UNEP’s written responses to iTAP comments, it is envisaged that by providing tangible bottom-up examples of EbA interventions and their integration into sub-national policies and plans, the project will create an enabling environment and catalyze their consideration also in national-level processes. The primary mechanism for this will be the NAP knowledge management platform, which is currently under development in Mauritania, where project reports and best practices guidance will be stored and dissemination. This centralised repository will enhance access to critical adaptation information for policymakers, practitioners and community-level stakeholders.

We acknowledge iTAP’s assessment of the project’s sustainable development potential as “medium to high”, and agree with iTAP’s concern regarding the potential risk of unsustainable extraction of groundwater. We acknowledge iTAP’s assessment of the needs of the recipient as “high”. We note that no specific recommendations are made in this regard.

We acknowledge iTAP’s assessment of country ownership as “medium to high”. Weaknesses will further strengthen MEDD’s project execution capacity, as will the specific capacity building included in the project to strengthen MEDD’s capacity for the design, execution and oversight of the Small Grants Facility.  

### Efficiency and effectiveness  
We acknowledge iTAP’s assessment of the project’s efficiency and effectiveness as “medium”, and agree on the need for a coordination mechanism for related initiatives. On this recommendation, please see our response under the “overall remarks” section.  

Regarding the government’s capacity to fund adaptation, although this currently low, with the increased priority allocated to addressing climate change and its improved integration in policies, plans and budgets, this is likely to improve. As for post-project sustainability, government investment in the maintenance of interventions is a realistic expectation in the Mauritanian context.  

Finally, we agree with the importance of identifying long-term financing sources for replenishing the SGF, to ensure its continued operation beyond the project timeframe.  

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:  
We have taken note of iTAP’s recommendations, and commit to addressing them as follows:  

#### Recommendation 1: Create a coordination mechanism to share lessons being learned and adaptive management practices being undertaken in response to evolving climate hazards  
**Response:** Under the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process, in which UNEP is a Delivery Partner, a multi-stakeholder coordination mechanism for climate change adaptation in Mauritania will be established. To ensure that coordination between initiatives isn’t limited to the duration of this project, a working group under the NAP coordination platform will be established by UNEP and MEDD to this effect. This will be used to share lessons learnt between the parallel co-finance projects, other projects in the Great Green Wall financed by GCF, and other technical and financial partners of Government of Mauritania.  

#### Recommendation 2: Include in the environmental and social management plan the risk of potential overextraction of groundwater (…), with an added project subactivity to establish a governance system (…)  
**Response:** Before project inception, the ESMP will be revised to include risk mitigation activities focused on determining and enforcing pumping times and volumes, monitoring changes in water tables and restricting pumping if needed, as well as on the training of Water User Groups (WUGs) on these specific aspects. The description of the WUGs roles will be updated in the FP and Annex 2, to specifically highlight these responsibilities, and they will also be included in the TOR for WUGs. Budget for these additional mitigation measures (including for the necessary technical expertise) will be allocated through a budget revision.

# Annex 8: Gender Action Plan

## Acronyms and abbreviations
- CEDAW: Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women  
- EbA: Ecosystem-based adaptation  
- FGM: Female genital mutilation  
- GBV: Gender-based violence  
- PMU: Project Management Unit  

## 1. Part 1: Gender Analysis

### 1.1. Introduction
The overall objective of the ‘Strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations’ project is to contribute to improved resilience of local and vulnerable communities in four regional hubs in northern Mauritania. To fulfil the project’s design requirements, this gender analysis was undertaken in accordance with GCF guidelines to inform the project design on gender barriers, constraints, and opportunities for women to benefit from the project’s activities. In addition, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) will ensure women and other marginalized groups are included in the project’s activities. The GAP is based on the proposed project’s logical framework and is focused on avoiding and minimising the negative impacts of the project’s activities on local communities and maximising the positive impacts and social benefits, particularly for women and marginalised groups.

### 1.2. Methodology
This gender analysis primarily used qualitative data collection methods, including informant interviews with multiple stakeholders and partners (See Annex 7: stakeholder engagement plan) as well as mixed community consultations. The method involving open community meetings proved particularly effective as it allowed for the provision of information by individuals through open discussions, accompanied by immediate expressions of approval or disapproval and corroborated by testimonies or cases from other group members. This facilitated an accurate representation of the gender-related challenges faced by their respective communities.

The gender analysis has also made use of:  
i) A desktop review of available literature;  
ii) Gender equality studies conducted by research institutions and donor agencies; and  
iii) Details made available by the government of Mauritania.  

The questions posed to the community members focused on the lived experiences of individuals and communities regarding the effects of climate change and their gendered responses to these challenges. This data collection method was preceded and supplemented by a review of existing literature on gender dynamics and equality in Mauritania — particularly within the rural and agricultural sectors — to establish a framework aligned with the project’s objectives and anticipated impact. The findings of the gender analysis (Part 1) form the basis of the GAP (part 2).

### 1.3. Women’s rights in Mauritania
Mauritania has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), and its legal system includes four areas of law that impact women differently: Islamic law; Customary African law; Customary Arabic-Bedouin and Berber law; and (modern) Civil law. Women's rights in marriage remain uncertain, particularly under Islamic and customary African law.

In 2005, Mauritania ratified the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol), pledging to promote women’s rights and eliminate violence against them. However, at the national level, no comprehensive law addressing violence against women exists to date. Specific laws address particular forms of violence, such as rape and female genital mutilation, but offer no legal protection or recourse for women experiencing other types of violence, like spousal abuse and sexual harassment. The absence of a comprehensive legal framework

The high illiteracy rate among women often leaves them unaware of their rights, and the distribution of

## General situation of women in households

Globally, progress has been made regarding  women’s rights; however, substantial effort is needed  to 
achieve gender equality in Mauritania. In 2021, the Gender Development Index for Mauritania was 0.890 
which categorises Mauritania in group five, the lowest group that comprises countries with low equality 
in HDI achievements between women and men . In addition, the Gender Inequality Index was 0.632 
which ranks Mauritania 161st out of 170 countries.

The legal age for marriage is 18 for both women and men (Personal Code, Art. 6), with child marriage 
being prohibited and punishable by imprisonment and fines (Juvenile Justice Code, Art. 41). However, 
child marriage is widespread in the country , especially among rural, economically disadvantaged, and 
less educated girls who are married for their families’ social and financial benefit. Nationally, there has 
 been a trend of women marrying later. Some women in the age bracket 40 -49 married at the average 
age of 16, whereas women in the 25–29-year age bracket married at 19. There is also an increase in 
age for the birth of the first child. These changes are closely linked to the increase in the level of 
women’s education. However, women of reproductive age (15-49 years) often face barriers with respect 
to their sexual and reproductive health and rights: from 2019–2021, only 29% of women had their family 
planning needs met with modern methods, and 37% of women aged 20–24 years were married or in a 
union before age 18. The adolescent birth rate is 78 per 1,000 women aged 15-19 as of 2021, up from 
71 per 1,000 in 2011.

Under the Personal Status Code, marriage is defined as a union between a man and a woman and 
requires the consent of both parties (Art. 1). However, consent is loosely defined, with “the silence of 
a young girl” considered as consent (Art. 9). According to the law, a legal marriage comprises the bride 
and groom, the guardian or the weli of the bride (who serves to represent her in concluding the marriage 
contract), the dowry or the sadaq, and the consent of both parties (Art. 5). Polygamy is legal, with men 
allowed up to four wives with the consent of existing spouses (Art. 28). Polygamy is rarely practiced 
among Moor communities but is prevalent among other ethnic groups in the country. Under customary 
law, which is not recognised by the state, a woman's first marriage requires parental consent , and  
Muslim women are expected to marry within their faith, whereas Muslim men do not face the same 
restriction. Cultural taboos against the mixing of social classes or inter-caste unions result in marriages 
among members within the same clan. The husband is considered the head of the household.

# 4

(Personal Status Code, Art. 1) and is the legal authority of the household who can make decisions on behalf of family without the wife’s consent.

Although women and men both have inheritance rights, women’s entitlements differs considerable from men’s. Under Sharia law, a woman inherits only half of what a man does (Personal Status Code, Art. 253-259). Accordingly, a daughter may inherit only half of the property entitled to her male sibling. When a family has only daughters and no sons, the inheritance of the deceased father is first distributed among his brothers and other members of the family and the remainder to his daughters and his wife.

Women face additional challenges in retaining property (both land and non-land assets) in divorce and widowhood, where their right to ownership of assets is often limited to objects related to women’s work and daily life such as cooking and cleaning utensils (Personal Code, Art. 73-74). In some cases, the family of the deceased father is granted full custody of the child (rather than to the living mother), allowing his family sole rights over the child’s inheritance.

## 1.5. Division of labour

Rural women play an essential role in agriculture. Although only 25.4% of women in the country are classified as employed, many more participate in unpaid work in the informal sector and agriculture. Women are responsible for vital tasks related to food provision and household maintenance. They are involved in all phases of the agricultural cycle, from planting and weeding to the harvesting, conservation and maintenance of fields, storage, processing and marketing of produce. Women play an important role in market gardening and in milk product processing. In the fishery sector, women also contribute to the processing and marketing of fish and fish products. Women participate in almost all aspects of agricultural and livestock activities, while men primarily focus on land clearing and cultivating specific crops such as wetland rice. Men also take care of the herding and watering of large animals, while the curing of skins, the processing of milk products, and the care of small ruminants and poultry is the task of women. In addition, women undertake household tasks such as food processing and preparation, fetching water and collecting wood. Women are also the practitioners of traditional medicine.

Although women in certain areas were traditionally responsible for rice cultivation, wetland rice cultivation is now predominantly handled by men, with women mainly focusing on irrigation, storage and marketing. In some areas, both men and women participate in the construction of small dams. Major decisions on land transfer and agricultural investments are generally made by the men in the extended family. While women generally do not own land or livestock, they usually make decisions regarding their work responsibilities and the income, derived from their activities.

## 1.6. Impact of Climate Change

As stated in Mauritania’s National Adaptation Programme of Action to Climate Change (2004) and, more recently, in the country’s first Nationally Determined Contribution (2021), climate change impacts men and women differently, with women often disproportionately affected by adverse impacts. For example, the increasing frequency of drought in Mauritania results in women having to walk longer distances for water and firewood or develop new income-generating activities, such as weaving and tie-dying. Men and women have different access to financial, social, or political resources and generally hold distinct roles and responsibilities within households. Women tend to balance domestic tasks such as cooking, cleaning, and childrearing in addition to agricultural responsibilities. It is common for women to maintain kitchen gardens for household consumption, while men maintain cash crops. Finally, because of gender inequalities with regards to financial assets, women tend to be responsible for poultry

# 5

The West African Sahel has experienced periods of long -term decline in rainfall that was intensified by  
shorter periods of severe drought. In addition to the high human toll, this series of droughts contributed  
to a decrease in species diversification, tree density and plant coverage throughout the region. Climate  
variability challenges dependence on  natural resources for sustenance, particularly when alternative  
livelihood options are limited in rural areas. Insufficient water sources force women and girls to travel  
farther for water, exposing them to potential harm, violence and the social repercussions resulting from  
sexual assault. The health concerns that arise from carrying heavy water containers over long distances  
include dehydration, back and spinal injuries, fatigue, dehydration, and reproductive complications. This  
extra work also detracts from time that women would otherwise spend on productive activities, which  
sometimes results in decreased agricultural productivity, household food security, and overall household  
income. This problem is termed ‘time poverty’ and highlights the ways women experience less free time  
available than men as they are often involved in very time-consuming non-remunerated household work  
on top of outside work to supplement their household income. This means that they may have less  
flexibility to diversify their activities far from the home or participate in projects designed to improve their  
resilience. To compensate for this time  allocation problem with regards to water collection, some  
households assign girl children the responsibility of collecting water, forcing them to  leave school and  
contribute to household labour, thereby perpetuating  social inequity. In addition, women are often  
excluded as climate change resources prioritise information dissemination methods accommodate  
men's abilities and needs. However, emerging literature on women’s adaptation strategies, such as new  
technologies and climate -smart agriculture practices and collective action and information sharing  
strategies, emphasises the importance of recognising women’s knowledge and preferred ways of  
learning across diverse contexts. Development agencies have started incorporating these  
considerations into their programme initiatives23.  

## 1.7. Access to land  

Land rights are a particular concern in Mauritania, as few women own land. In the traditional economy,  
women, youth, and the servant class  do not possess land . Decisions on and -use are generally  
determined by men and the elderly. Land governance in Mauritania represents a major component of  
sustainable development and poverty reduction. Implementing effective land policies is  crucial for  
reducing poverty by stimulating economic prosperity and fostering self-sufficiency. Viable and inclusive  
land governance stimulates the consolidation of social peace and the inclusion of women and the poor  
in an open discussion about access to land security. Although many Mauritanian women desire land  
ownership, only 2,146 of 27,000 title deeds registered at the national level, or slightly less than 8%, are  
held by women (Table 1). Despite women’s limited access to permits and land titles, authorities have  
not yet adopt ed an affirmative action policy that promotes increasing women's access to land.  
Investigations conducted with the help of officials and an analysis of the Land Tenure Office database  
showed that most of the data had not yet been disaggregated by gender. However, in 2015, the Land  
Tenure Office made efforts to allow  data disaggregation by identifying names by gender, notably  
creating a database that includes gender as a key variable24.

### 1.8. Access to and control over resources

In Mauritania, considerable gender disparities exist in men’s and women’s access to and control over resources, largely as a result of entrenched socio-cultural norms and legal frameworks. Women in rural areas have restricted access to land, water and credit, despite legal provisions recognising their rights. For example, less than 15% of women in Mauritania own land, and even fewer have formal land titles, which limits their ability to access agricultural inputs and loans. This gender gap is compounded by traditional customs that prioritise male inheritance of land and resources, further marginalising women’s participation in agricultural productivity and economic decision-making.

A case study from the southern region of Mauritania highlights these challenges, where men largely dominate decision-making processes related to natural resources, including water management and grazing rights. This control affects women’s ability to engage in productive activities such as farming or livestock rearing, which are necessary for household income and food security. However, gender-sensitive initiatives, such as the World Bank-funded Community-Based Watershed Management Project, have demonstrated some progress in empowering women by improving their access to water for irrigation and livestock.

### 1.9. Gender and agricultural livelihoods

Agriculture in Mauritania is characterised by gender-specific roles, with men and women participating in different tasks within the sector. Women in rural areas are involved in subsistence farming, food processing and livestock management, all of which contribute to household food security. However, they face obstacles in fully participating in and benefiting from the agricultural economy. Women make up nearly 43% of the agricultural labour force, yet they have limited access to land, financial resources, training and agricultural inputs. Less than 10% of women have access to formal agricultural extension services, which limits their capacity to improve productivity and resilience to climate impacts.

## 1.10. Participation of women in politics

Under the Constitution, women and men have equal rights to hold public office. The government has 
instituted several measures to ensure and increase the participation of women in the public and political 
space. One such measure is the electoral law introduced in 2006 that requires a minimum of 20% of all 
municipal council seats to be reserved for women. In 2012, the country reformed the electoral law to 
add 51 new seats to the National Assembly, of which 20 are reserved for women. In 2013, the 
government introduced financial incentives for political parties that nominate more women than required 
by the quota for national assembly seats. As of February 2021, only 20.3% of seats in parliament were 
held by women. 

In 2015, women members of parliament (MPs) in Mauritania created an action plan to advance gender 
equality. This plan followed a  training session in Nouakchott on strategic planning on the work of the 
women’s caucus in parliament involving approximately 40 female MPs, civil society organi sations and 
government officials. The workshop, jointly organi sed by the parliamentary women’s group REFPAM, 
IPU and the National Assembly, included training on frameworks for protecting women’s rights. Priority 
areas for 2016 include promoting new laws to combat violence against women and girls, and 
strengthening female MPs through study visits, training activities and other projects. 

Despite these measures, women remain underrepresented in public service, particularly in ministerial, 
judiciary and foreign service  roles. Limited access to education and employment, the lack of financial 
support for female political candidates, and the traditional stereotypes of women’s role in society 
compared to men contribute to the low level of female political participation. Women from Haratine 
communities, in particular, have limited or no access to public and political life.

## 1.11. Access to justice

The justice system in Mauritania consists of courts that apply the principles of both civil and Sharia law. 
Although the Personal Status Code outlines provisions for the application of these two legal systems, 
judicial officials do not always abide by it. Under Sharia law, a woman’s testimony holds half of the 
evidentiary weight of that of a man. Moreover, in cases of r ape, judges can accuse the victim of 
fornication outside marriage (zina), and theoretically, imprison the victim on these grounds.  

Regarding legal proceedings, many courts conduct  their sessions in Arabic, and interpreters are not 
always available. In addition, there is inadequate legal aid and counselling available to women, a lack 
of training among legal and judicial officers on women’s rights and gender sensitivity, and a reliance on 
conciliators (mouslihines) to settle cases outside of the formal justice system. Consequently, certain
